### 1. ★★★★☆ Oil News: Ceasefire Hopes Test Crude as Hormuz Risk Keeps Brent Supported - FXEmpire

Oil News: Ceasefire Hopes Test Crude as Hormuz Risk Keeps Brent Supported
Proposed Russian diesel shipments and ceasefire hopes eased immediate supply pressure, pushing gasoil futures down 3%.
Ukraine is still awaiting ceasefire details and Russia has not commented, leaving the energy deal unverified.
Hormuz tanker attacks, Saudi strikes and Gulf production restarts pull the crude supply outlook in opposite directions.
Peace Headlines Test Crude as Hormuz Risk Holds the Floor
Crude starts the week caught between two stories that do not cancel each other out. The Russia-Ukraine ceasefire and a promise of Russian diesel gave sellers a headline to work with Monday. The Strait of Hormuz attacks and the Saudi airport strikes were not part of any deal.
Sellers have the diplomatic angle, but confirmed barrels and safe shipping lanes are still missing from the trade.
December WTI crude oil futures finished 1.34% higher last week. Brent held above $100 per barrel despite the latest push to ease refined-product tightness.
Next up Natural Gas and Oil Forecast: WTI Tests $91.40, Can Brent Break $106 and NG Reach $3.35?
Weekly December WTI Crude Oil Futures Technical Analysis
December WTI crude oil futures opened the week under pressure early Monday after finishing 1.34% higher last week. The main trend is up according to the weekly swing chart. A trade through $96.41 will signal a resumption of the uptrend. The main trend will change to down on a break through $71.00.
The minor trend is up. A trade through last week’s high at $92.14 will turn $86.00 into a new minor bottom.
The minor range is $96.41 to $86.00. Its pivot at $91.21 is likely to control the direction of the market this week.
The nearest support is a pair of 50% levels at $83.71 to $81.69. With the main trend up, buyers could show up on a pullback into this area. A failure to hold $81.69 could trigger an acceleration to the downside with $76.02 to $71.21 the next major target zone. Inside this zone is the 52-week moving average at $71.95. This indicator has been controlling the trend since late January.
Every new Brent Oil analysis as it publishes, today&#x27;s technical signal and key levels, live price — on one page.
Weekly December Brent Crude Oil Futures Technical Analysis
December Brent crude oil futures are lower early Monday. The market is trading inside last week’s range, suggesting trader indecision. The main trend is up according to the weekly swing chart. A trade through $105.92 will signal a resumption of the uptrend. The main trend will change to down on a trade through $75.77.
The minor trend is also up. A trade through $93.68 will change the minor trend to down and shift momentum to the downside.
A trade through $105.92 will put $113.14 on the radar. On the downside, the first pivot price to watch is $99.80. This pivot is likely to control the direction of the market this week. Buyers could step in on a pullback into this level, but if it fails as support, Brent futures will be vulnerable to a correction into the main trend 50% level at $94.41 and the minor swing bottom at $93.68.
Russian Diesel Deal Takes Some Heat Out of the Trade
Trump announced Russian diesel headed for global markets. More than 300,000 metric tons immediately, another 500,000 metric tons in November, and 1 million metric tons after that. Gasoil futures dropped 3% to $1,436 per barrel on the news.
Global diesel consumption runs near 29 million barrels per day, so the announced volumes ease some of the tightness without rebalancing the market on their own. Additionally, the International Energy Agency is pressing members to assess whether additional diesel releases are needed and to accelerate pledged volumes that have not been delivered.
The ceasefire is the same trade. Trump said both sides agreed to stop hitting energy facilities. Ukraine said it was waiting for details. Russia did not comment.
Hormuz and Saudi Attacks Keep the Supply Risk Alive
T he Middle East risk has not gone away. The Houthis claimed responsibility for a strike on Riyadh’s international airport Saturday. Saudi authorities reported another attack on the airport Sunday. Trump said the United States was considering joining Saudi strikes on the Houthis.
The shipping story is just as important. United Kingdom Maritime Trade Operations reported that one tanker was struck by a drone near Sohar anchorage in Oman and another was damaged moving through the Strait of Hormuz. More attacks this month threaten the recovery in Middle East crude exports seen in September.
None of that changed with the ceasefire discussion. A Russia-Ukraine deal on energy facilities does not extend to the Red Sea or the Gulf shipping lanes.
Shell, Chevron and BP were redeploying personnel after Hurricane Isaias made landfall Saturday. Gulf of Mexico production coming back takes away the supply story that helped WTI late last week. That puts more near-term pressure on the WTI side of the market.
Goldman Sachs said high U.S.-Europe shipping costs have widened the WTI-Brent spread. Goldman raised its December Brent forecast to $90 per barrel from $85 and estimated the geopolitical risk premium at just over $20 per barrel. The bank forecast WTI at $81 in December 2026 and $73 in 2027.
The ceasefire has not been verified and the diesel has not been loaded, but Gulf production restarts are already adding supply back into the market. On the other side, the Hormuz shipping risk and Saudi airport attacks are not part of any negotiation. Sellers had the diplomatic headline Monday. The tanker attacks near Hormuz kept them from finishing the job.
WTI’s pivot at $91.21 controls the week. Buyers are taking offers above that level. The next support sits at $83.71 to $81.69.
Brent held above $100 with $99.80 underneath as the floor for the risk premium trade. The Hormuz premium and the wider WTI spread are both in the price.

Addressing the conference virtually, Ukrainian president Volodymyr Zelenskyy commented on Trump’s “sudden but very important” energy ceasefire declaration over the weekend.
He said it is clear that Putin “does not read Truth social”, the site that the US president posted his ceasefire declaration on.
As we have been reporting, Russia’s strikes continued despite the announcement. “Immediately after the post Russia launched a targeted strike on a power plant in Kharkiv, causing further damage to our energy system,” Zelenskyy said, noting that Ukraine is facing a wave of escalatory attacks from Russia ahead of the winter.

In other news, German Chancellor Friedrich Merz’s government came under renewed pressure this morning after a key minister from its junior coalition partner, the SPD, unexpectedly quit.
Baerbel Bas, labour minister and co-leader of the centre-left SPD, cited personal reasons for her resignation, saying she needed time to grieve after the death of her stepdaughter.
But she also criticised Merz’s conservative Christian Democratic Union (CDU), which is in crisis after heavy recent electoral defeats, notably the far-right AfD’s victory in Saxony-Anhalt last month .
“I must unfortunately note that the public attacks against me – regrettably including those from within the ranks of our coalition partner – have continued to intensify during this difficult time for me personally,” Bas said. She will reportedly replaced as labour minister by Alexander Schweitzer, one of the SPD’s vice-chairs.
The resignation comes amid turmoil for the government, which is grappling with a sluggish economy and low poll ratings.
Bas intends to retain her seat as an MP for Duisburg, a city at the heart of the former Ruhr mining region in northern Germany. Photograph: Maryam Majd/Reuters The Nobel Prize for economics has been awarded to Robert E Hall and Michael Woodford, for their work on the “microeconomic foundations of macroeconomic stabilisation”.
[Reminder: As we flagged earlier , Woodford was among the predicted winners – he’s one of the architects of modern forward guidance, the idea that central banks can control the economy through influencing public expectations about inflation, rather than just through interest rates.]
The S veriges Riksbank Prize in Economic Sciences in Memory of Alfred Nobel is worth 12 million Swedish kronor (£900,000), plus the glory of course, and the knowledge that every pronouncement you make now comes with the authority of a Nobel Prize winner.
Nobel prize for economics to be awarded to Hall and Woodford – business live
Zelenskyy said Trump’s energy ceasefire announcement caught him by surprise, first hearing about it through the media.
No, we did not know about that. We’ve learned that from the media as well that the president of the United States has secured an energy ceasefire.
I thought perhaps their team reached out to the Russians. I think they are in constant contact in a certain format.
During a Q&A with journalists, Zelenskyy said he’s ready to find ways to alter the constitution to be able to hold elections “very quickly” if a “full” energy ceasefire is implemented. He said this may require “difficult (legislative) solutions”.
“As of now, it is very difficult because of the security level in our cities and villages and communities. It is also impossible now because of our military. More than a million people who must vote – but how will they vote while being on the frontline?”
Since Russia’s full-scale invasion of Ukraine in February 2022, the country has been under martial law, meaning elections are suspended.
But Donald Trump , under pressure ahead of the November elections, said on Saturday it was time for Ukraine to get a new president, suggesting Zelenskyy has not done enough to end the war and blaming him for high diesel prices in the US, even though they have largely been affected by his war on Iran.
The Baltic states have reacted sceptically to Donald Trump’s diesel deal with Vladimir Putin and the US president’s so-called energy ceasefire.
Lithuania’s foreign minister, Keʂtutis Budrys, said Trump’s diesel deal with Vladimir Putin was not helpful. Budrys said:
This deal doesn’t help, neither the crisis of the oil products, nor the unity within the transatlantic community when it concerns Russia. And it is not the European way. Our way is to increase pressure because we already see how it works.
His Estonian counterpart, Margus Tsakhna, gave a similar assessment: “I don’t think that in real life this diesel deal is actually going to change anything on the oil price or diesel price in the market.” He drew a link between US domestic politics and the upcoming midterms.
Estonia’s foreign minister was also sceptical about Trump’s self-proclaimed energy ceasefire. Tsakhna said:
[The] energy ceasefire is not working as President Zelenskyy has said as well. And Russia is really putting more attacks against Ukraine. So what we need to do in Europe, we need to focus on what we can do.
He cited EU sanctions against 1,600 Russian individuals and entities that are due to be signed off later today by the bloc’s 27 foreign ministers, the largest ever-listing of its kind.
Zelenskyy says Ukraine is waiting for the specific details of the “conversation” between the US and Russia after the latest peace talks.
On Friday, negotiators from the US, Ukraine and European countries also held talks in Miami aimed ⁠at ​reaching agreement on a unified peace proposal ​in the coming weeks that could be presented to Russia.
Zelenskyy said in his address that the energy ceasefire could be a “major step” toward ending the war if Trump makes sure Putin honours it.
The Ukrainian leader says Kyiv needs the US to apply real pressure on the Kremlin, not sanction relief or “concessions”.
Volodymyr Zelenskyy speaks to the audience via an online video call during the opening ceremony at the Warsaw Security Forum. Photograph: Omar Marques/Getty Images Zelenskyy said Russia is trying to tactically use the period of cold weather against Ukraine , targeting the country’s power plants and power grids.
“This winter if Russia does not agree to an energy ceasefire, we will respond,” Zelenskyy said, adding that he supports Trump’s energy ceasefire proposal, but vows that if Russia doesn’t agree to it, then Ukraine “will respond”.
“If Russia wants us to stop our long-range strikes against its oil refineries and oil exports, then Russia must stop its strikes against our energy system and critical infrastructure, and stop killing our people. This is only fair,” Zelenskyy said.
“We are ready to respect an energy ceasefire if Russia is ready to do the same. We agree, if they agree. And we have already shown that we mean it. Last night, Russian oil refineries were not burning but at midnight a Russian jet powered Shahed strike against a production facility in Ukraine.”
Addressing the conference virtually, Ukrainian president Volodymyr Zelenskyy commented on Trump’s “sudden but very important” energy ceasefire declaration over the weekend.
He said it is clear that Putin “does not read Truth social”, the site that the US president posted his ceasefire declaration on.
As we have been reporting, Russia’s strikes continued despite the announcement. “Immediately after the post Russia launched a targeted strike on a power plant in Kharkiv, causing further damage to our energy system,” Zelenskyy said, noting that Ukraine is facing a wave of escalatory attacks from Russia ahead of the winter.
Speaking at the security conference in Warsaw, Polish prime minister Donald Tusk warned that Russia wants to “dominate” Ukraine and poses a grave threat to Nato.
Russia wants to ruin the existing security architecture in Europe to make sure that Russian domination comes back to where it was before 1989. We have no doubt about it.
Tusk says his country, Poland, a Nato and EU member, has heavily invested in defence to be prepared to counter future security challenges.
Tusk said despite the hybrid threats from Russia he is “optimistic” when he thinks of the high level of military readiness for “different scenarios”.
“This is the moment when the entire west, Europe , the United States must show Russia’s Putin that we are ready for a deterrence policy with even harder measures – and I mean mainly sanctions,” he told the conference.
Tusk told parliament last month that intelligence assessments suggested Russia might launch an attack with drones or missiles on Nato territory in the coming months, as part of efforts to prove that “article 5 is more theoretical than practical”.
Polish prime minister Donald Tusk speaks during the Warsaw Security Forum in Warsaw, Poland. Photograph: Aleksandra Szmigiel/Reuters The EU foreign policy chief Kaja Kallas said the EU would welcome a truce on striking energy infrastructure between Ukraine and Russia “when it holds”, after US President Donald Trump announced a ceasefire, which Volodymyr Zelenskyy said was “news to me”.
Arriving at a meeting of EU foreign ministers on Monday, Kallas said:
We welcome the energy ceasefire when it holds. We haven’t yet seen that also Russia will stick to it.
Kallas said she wanted to see “people” included in any ceasefire. But an energy truce also had to cover more than just oil refineries in the two countries “where Ukraine has one and Russia has plenty, so it’s definitely in the benefit of the Russians.”
She did not respond directly to a question about Trump’s diesel deal with Vladimir Putin , saying: “That is why we are, of course, continuing our path. We are now adopting 1,600 listings because we only see that pressure on Russia would work.”
Kaja Kallas speaks to the press during the EU foreign affairs council meeting in Luxembourg. Photograph: Olivier Hoslet/EPA
The EU will finalise later today sanctions against 1,600 Russian individuals and entities, targeting the military-industrial complex and officials involved in “sham elections” in Ukraine’s occupied territories.
Kallas said EU sanctions “would work so much better if our partners from the UK and from the US would be aligned with us”. She went on to say that the UK was aligned, “but if the US would be aligned, if they would enact the Graham bill regarding Russia, we could really put the pressure so that Russia will finish this war”.
Trump signed the Lindsey O Graham Sanctioning Russia and Iran Act into law earlier this month, which is aimed at depleting Russia’s ability to continue funding the war by targeting its natural gas and oil revenues, as well as countries that continue to buy Russian energy.
Donald Tusk, who is one of the staunchest supporters of Kyiv, said for the entire “western world” security is “no longer an academic problem” or a subject of journalistic intrigue, it is a “very concrete result of Russian aggression on Ukraine”.
Tusk said anybody who suggests that Ukraine is responsible for the war “gives a valuable gift to the aggressor”.
The prime minister’s comments come after Donald Trump blamed Volodymyr Zelenskkyy for stalled peace efforts over the weekend, and said he thought it was time for Ukraine to get a new president.
Poland’s prime minister, Donald Tusk , has just begun addressing the security conference in Warsaw. I will bring you any key lines. You can watch it here live .
The biggest prize in economics, the Sveriges Riksbank Prize in Economic Sciences in Memory of Alfred Nobel, will be announced at the Royal Swedish Academy of Sciences in Stockholm from 10.45am UK time .
Leading the betting on Polymarket , at 30% this morning, is David Autor , professor of economics at the Massachusetts Institute of Technology . Autor is a labour economist, who has examined how technology changes the world of work.
Another potential winner is Ariél Pakes , the Thomas Professor of Economics at Harvard University , currently at a 19% chance on Polymarket.
Pakes’s work has focused on developing methods to empirically analyse market responses to environmental and policy changes, such as mergers and deregulation.
Third, at 12%, is Susan Athey , the Economics of Technology Professor at Stanford Graduate School of Business . Athey , the former consulting chief economist for Microsoft , was the first woman to win the John Bates Clark Medal (awarded to the top economist under the age of 40).
There’s also some interest in American macroeconomist and monetary theorist Michael Woodford (currently at 10%) – he’s one of the architects of modern forward guidance, the idea that central banks can control the economy through influencing public expectations about inflation, rather than just through interest rates.
There have been multiple reports of overnight Russian attacks on Ukraine , affecting energy infrastructure and hitting civilian areas.
Oleksandr Hanzha, head of the Dnipropetrovsk regional military administration, said this morning that Russia attacked three districts of the region “more than 10 times” with artillery and drones. The strikes caused fires in the Petrykivka and Mykolaiv areas of the Dniprovskyi district , Hanzha added in his Telegram post, which noted there were no injuries from the attacks.
Residents in several streets in Izyum , south-east of Kharkiv in eastern Ukraine , were reportedly left without power after a Russian attack damaged power grids.
Meanwhile, the governor of Ukraine’s Zhytomyr region, Vitaliy Bunechko, said Russian forces have been “repeatedly shelling” the north of the region “since the evening”.
“Railway and energy infrastructure facilities in the Korosten district were under attack,” he added in a Telegram post this morning, noting that the fires were quickly extinguished. Bunechko said there was no information about the victims or their injuries yet.
⁠At ⁠least 12 ​people ⁠were ⁠injured in ​several ‌Russian ‌attacks ‌on Ukraine’s Kharkiv ‌on ​Monday morning, ⁠mayor Ihor ​Terekhov ​said. Here are some of the latest images being sent to us over the newswires from Ukraine’s second largest city:
Firefighters work at the site of an apartment building damaged during a Russian drone strike in Kharkiv, Ukraine, on 12 October 2026. Photograph: Sofiia Gatilova/Reuters View image in fullscreen
Residents and rescuers take a shelter in a basement of an apartment building during a threat of another Russian drone strike in Kharkiv. Photograph: Vyacheslav Madiyevskyy/Reuters We are expecting Volodymyr Zelenskyy to speak at the annual security conference in Warsaw alongside Polish prime minister Donald Tusk from 8.30am to 9.30am UK time this morning. Stay with us as we will bring you the key lines from what the two leaders say.
The fast-moving row between Washington and Kyiv began on Friday when Trump announced he was lifting sanctions on Russian diesel exports . He said the move would reduce global and domestic fuel prices before next month’s US midterm elections.
A furious Zelenskyy called the decision “weak” and said it would generate “billions” in extra revenue for Putin, enabling him to wage war “even more intensely”. An equally irate Trump said Zelenskyy should “damn well stop” hitting Russian refineries. Ukraine needed a “new leader” , he said on Saturday.
Map of Ukraine with Russian advances and territory. Responding to Trump, Zelenskyy said it was up to Ukrainians to choose “one or another leader”. He told ABC News he was willing to accept Trump’s demands and to organise elections, but only if Putin agreed to a “total” 60-day ceasefire.
The New York Times has reported that US officials have offered the Kremlin lucrative economic incentives in exchange for a ceasefire. They reportedly include releasing billions in Russian sovereign assets that are frozen. The Trump administration also revisited a plan to sell parts of Russia’s energy company, Lukoil. The multibillion deal would benefit Middle Eastern investors, the paper reported , some with “business ties” to US negotiators. You can read the full story here:
Trump announces partial ceasefire in Ukraine war as Zelenskyy says deal is ‘news to me’
We are restarting our live coverage of the latest developments in Europe after Donald Trump ’s announcement of what he called an energy ceasefire in the Russia-Ukraine war.
The Ukrainian president, Volodymyr Zelenskyy , said on Sunday evening that Russian strikes hit Ukrainian energy facilities despite the US president having claimed both sides had agreed to stop hitting each other’s energy infrastructure.
In a post on X, Zelenskyy said a drone had hit a Kyiv power plant on Sunday, leading to power outages in the Ukrainian capital. Shortly after, he said there had been a strike on a power plant in the north-eastern city of Kharkiv , adding: “Ukrainian skies have been infested with Russian ‘shaheds’ all day”, referring to a type of Iranian-made drone commonly used in the conflict.
Ukrainian president Volodymyr Zelenskyy says he was caught by surprise by Trump’s ceasefire announcement. Photograph: Dan Bashakov/AP Meanwhile, Russian ⁠attacks killed six people ⁠in two Ukrainian ⁠cities ​on Sunday, including a strike ⁠on a grocery store in Kharkiv, ​where ‌three people died ‌and 20 were ‌injured, Ukrainian officials said. Two children were ‌among the injured, the regional governor said. In western ​Ukraine, ‌a Russian strike ​killed ​three people, officials ​said.
On Sunday morning Trump had announced that “effective immediately, there is an ENERGY CEASEFIRE in the War between Russia and Ukraine”. “Both parties have agreed. Do not break it!” he wrote on his Truth Social platform, without further details.
Zelenskyy expressed surprise at the announcement, saying the deal was “news to me”, while Russia has still not confirmed its agreement. Zelenskyy said on Telegram yesterday that: “Ukraine is ready to refrain from striking Russian energy infrastructure – and we count on the same readiness from Russia.”
Nato Supreme Allied Cmdr Europe Alexus Grynkewich is due to deliver a warning to Russia at a security conference in Warsaw over the alliance’s ability to counter any attack from Moscow.
The Lithuanian ⁠foreign minister Kestutis ​Budrys said the EU should designate the Russian ‌intelligence agency GRU ​as ⁠a ​terrorist organisation.
The Nobel memorial prize in economics, the final award in this year’s cycle, will be announced at the Royal Swedish Academy of Sciences in Stockholm from 10.45am UK time.
Trump’s surprise ceasefire announcement came two days after he announced a controversial agreement to buy Russian diesel fuel – temporarily lifting US sanactions – prompting outrage from Ukraine and its European allies, who said it would embolden Moscow.
The Trump administration is threatening to stop the procurement system that provides Ukraine with weapons in order to press Kyiv to halt its deep strikes on Russian energy infrastructure, the Financial Times has quoted officials in Europe as saying. They said the US was lobbying European capitals and Kyiv in an attempt to end the attacks on Russian oil refineries.

Two More Oil Tankers Attacked Near Strait of Hormuz
Two more tankers were attacked near the Strait of Hormuz on Sunday, one in Hormuz and the other off the coast of Oman, Argus has reported , citing ship-tracking data and the Indian shipping minister.
One of the targets was a Suezmax loaded with Iraqi crude, which had set sail on October 8, the Indian top shipping official said. It was hit by an unknown projectile that caused fire on board and, at the time of writing, two sailors were still missing . The other tanker was a product carrier that was hit by a drone. That attack was reported earlier by the UK Maritime Trade Operations outlet.
"India reiterates the need for immediate de-escalation and a resolution to the ongoing conflict through dialogue and diplomacy," New Delhi's foreign ministry said. "Attacks on commercial vessels, seafarers and civilian infrastructure must cease. Freedom of navigation and the safe, unimpeded flow of maritime commerce through international waterways, in accordance with international law, must be upheld."
Iran's Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps has stepped up attacks on tankers in the Strait of Hormuz in the past couple of weeks, targeting vessels trying to transit the strait in dark mode, along a southern corridor near the coast of Oman.
The U.S. Navy is meanwhile striking Iranian-linked vessels, with the strikes heightening the already considerable shipping risk in the area. "All ships within the limits of the Strait of Hormuz that turn off their navigation and identification systems will be identified as hostile and will be dealt with severely and decisively," the IRGC's Navy forces said in a statement, as reported by Iranian state media.
The news of the latest attacks comes alongside a report about a so-called energy ceasefire between Russia and Ukraine, announced by President Trump over the weekend. Neither of the warring parties has confirmed the ceasefire, and Axios has since reported that it is a narrower refinery-only ceasefire proposal, but the news has pressured oil prices. Brent was trading above $103 per barrel after the news, and WTI was over $90 per barrel.

### 7. ★★★☆☆ Trump announces partial ceasefire in Ukraine war as Zelenskyy says deal is ‘news to me’

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T17:21:39+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Ukrainian president earlier rejected US call for Kyiv to stop bombing oil refineries, saying Moscow must first end attacks on energy infrastructureDonald Trump has announced an energy ceasefire between Ukraine and Russia, apparently without consulting Volodymyr Zelenskyy, who said the deal to stop long-range attacks was “news to me”.In a social media post, the US president claimed the two sides had reached an agreement. “Effective immediately, there is an ENERGY CEASEFIRE in the

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Aftermath of a Russian air strike in Kharkiv on Sunday. Photograph: Volodymyr Pavlov/Reuters
Aftermath of a Russian air strike in Kharkiv on Sunday. Photograph: Volodymyr Pavlov/Reuters
Trump announces partial ceasefire in Ukraine war as Zelenskyy says deal is ‘news to me’
Ukrainian president earlier rejected US call for Kyiv to stop bombing oil refineries, saying Moscow must first end attacks on energy infrastructure
Donald Trump has announced an energy ceasefire between Ukraine and Russia, apparently without consulting Volodymyr Zelenskyy , who said the deal to stop long-range attacks was “news to me”.
In a social media post, the US president claimed the two sides had reached an agreement. “Effective immediately, there is an ENERGY CEASEFIRE in the War between Russia and Ukraine . Both parties have agreed. Do not break it!” he wrote on Truth Social.
He offered no further details. Zelenskyy said he was surprised by the announcement but would agree to it if Vladimir Putin did the same. “If President Trump negotiated it with Putin – please tell us when the ceasefire begins. We are ready and we will accept it,” the Ukrainian president told the Axios website.
Earlier, Zelenskyy rebuffed Trump’s demand made on Saturday that Kyiv stop bombing Russian oil refineries. Zelenskyy said Moscow had to first halt its own “brutal” attacks on his country’s energy infrastructure.
Any deal to end long-range drone strikes by Ukraine on targets inside Russia would have to be “reciprocal”, he said. It should be backed by “real guarantees” – meaning punitive measures against Moscow from Ukraine’s allies, including the US.
The aftermath on Sunday of a Russian strike on Kharkiv that hit residential homes. Photograph: Vyacheslav Madiyevskyy/Reuters There was no sign that the Kremlin was observing Trump’s ceasefire. According to Zelenskyy, Russian drones took to the sky “throughout the day” on Sunday. There were also “unfortunately” several airstrikes, he said, including on energy infrastructure.
The Ukrainian president said the Russians hit thermal power station No 5 in the eastern city of Kharkiv – the latest of more than 200 strikes this week against electricity generation and transmission facilities in Ukraine. There were another 17 hits on gas and oil facilities, he added.
The fast-moving row between Washington and Kyiv began on Friday when Trump announced he was lifting sanctions on Russian diesel exports . He said the move would reduce global and domestic fuel prices before next month’s US midterm elections.
A furious Zelenskyy called the decision “weak” and said it would generate “billions” in extra revenue for Putin, enabling him to wage war “even more intensely”. An equally irate Trump said Zelenskyy should “damn well stop” hitting Russian refineries. Ukraine needed a “new leader” , he said on Saturday.
Map of Ukraine with Russian advances and territory Responding to Trump, Zelenskyy said it was up to Ukrainians to choose “one or another leader”. He told ABC News he was willing to accept Trump’s demands and to organise elections, but only if Putin agreed to a “total” 60-day ceasefire.
The bitter public feud is the most serious between the US and Ukraine since last year, when Trump berated Zelenskyy in the Oval Office .
The Financial Times, citing officials in Europe , reported on Sunday that the US is ⁠threatening ⁠to stop ​the procurement ⁠system that provides ⁠Ukraine ​with ‌weapons to ‌press Kyiv to halt its ‌deep strikes on ​Russian energy infrastructure.
The New York Times has reported that US officials have offered the Kremlin lucrative economic incentives in exchange for a ceasefire. They reportedly include releasing billions in Russian sovereign assets that are frozen. The Trump administration also revisited a plan to sell parts of Russia’s energy company, Lukoil. The multibillion deal would benefit Middle Eastern investors, the paper reported , some with “business ties” to US negotiators.
Medics help a woman injured in a Russian attack on a residential area in Zaporizhzhia, Ukraine on Saturday after overnight strikes. Photograph: Daryna Naumova/EPA On Saturday, Trump criticised Zelenskyy and said he had refused to make a deal to end the fighting. In response, Ukraine’s president laid the blame for Europe’s biggest war since 1945 on “Russia and Russia alone”. “It is possible to stop strikes on energy infrastructure as early as today, but Putin does not want to do so,” he said.
Ukrainian officials point out that the White House is demanding Kyiv give up its most effective weapon, without demanding any corresponding concessions from Russia. In recent weeks, Kyiv has dealt a major blow to Russia’s economy, striking oil complexes, warehouses and datacentres.
Russia’s own bombing campaign has been relentless. On Saturday it killed 23 people in the city of Zaporizhzhia, including four children, and hit a five-storey apartment block in the frontline town of Sloviansk. Zelenskyy said Moscow’s aerial campaign involved thousands of aerial bombs and jet-powered drones. It continued “unabated”, he said.
‘A life-or-death race’: the volunteers protecting Kyiv’s skies from new jet-powered drones
The head of Ukraine’s foreign affairs committee, Oleksandr Merezhko , said Trump’s “bullying” demand for Zelenskyy to quit had strengthened him at home “paradoxically”, after recent damaging corruption scandals. “Putin’s plan is to decapitate Zelenskyy and remove him as a wartime leader. Trump is siding with Putin,” Merezhko said.
He added: “I’m afraid that behind this situation is Trump’s worldview. He wants to do business with Putin. For him, Ukraine seems like an obstacle to be removed. Both Trump and Putin wish to pressure us into capitulation.”
Referring to the notorious 1939 agreement between Nazi Germany and the USSR, Merezhko said: “We are moving to a new Molotov-Ribbentrop pact and to a world of strongmen and spheres of influence.”
Merezhko said Ukraine would continue to destroy enemy oil refineries, against US wishes, because they funded Russia’s war machine and acknowledged that a cutoff of US intelligence sharing with Kyiv – threatened by the Trump administration – would “blind” Ukraine’s air defence systems. “This would be an act of complicity with Putin’s genocide. It would mean more dead civilians and dead children,” he said.
Ukrainian negotiators had been meeting in Florida with their American counterparts, Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner, when Trump announced the US was lifting sanctions on Russian diesel. Blindsided, the Ukrainians went home early. “It felt like a trap,” one stunned Ukrainian official told the Kyiv Independent newspaper .
On Sunday, Zelenskyy shored up support in a series of conversations with key allies including Canada’s prime minister, Mark Carney, the Polish leader, Donald Tusk, and the European Commission president, Ursula von der Leyen.
Earlier Zelenskyy spoke with the UK prime minister, Andy Burnham, France’s president, Emmanuel Macron, and the Finnish president, Alexander Stubb. Downing Street said Burnham recognised the last 24 hours had been “difficult” for Ukraine and “reiterated his complete solidarity”.
Downing Street said the two leaders agreed “to maintain pressure on Russia” and to strengthen the sanctions regime, despite Trump’s efforts to the contrary. “Russia should agree to an immediate energy ceasefire, ending its brutal strikes on Ukrainian infrastructure and its attacks on shipping in the Black Sea,” a statement added.
Speaking over the weekend, Zelenskyy said Trump’s decision empowered Putin and gave him “extra money for the war”, running into millions and “perhaps billions”. “It is certainly not fair. It is certainly not how partners should act. And, in my view, it is a weak decision,” he said.
Luke Harding’s Betrayal: Trump, Putin and the New Age of Conquest is published by Guardian Faber

### 8. ★★★☆☆ Houthi attack on Riyadh airport forces swift rethink of Trump’s new strategy

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Guardian World
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T13:23:02+00:00
- **发生了什么**: US president’s plan to park the wars in Iran and Yemen, while blaming Ukraine for diesel prices, looks unsustainableThe reckless missile attack on King Khalid international airport in Riyadh on Saturday did not just export the death and misery inside Yemen to Saudi Arabia, it also left Donald Trump forced to reconsider his diplomatic and political strategy designed to take him into the midterm elections next month.The US president’s plan had only been unveiled on Friday. He said

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Donald Trump has twice rejected Saudi appeals for help. Photograph: Roberto Schmidt/Getty Images
Donald Trump has twice rejected Saudi appeals for help. Photograph: Roberto Schmidt/Getty Images
Houthi attack on Riyadh airport forces swift rethink of Trump’s new strategy
US president’s plan to park the wars in Iran and Yemen, while blaming Ukraine for diesel prices, looks unsustainable
The reckless missile attack on King Khalid international airport in Riyadh on Saturday did not just export the death and misery inside Yemen to Saudi Arabia, it also left Donald Trump forced to reconsider his diplomatic and political strategy designed to take him into the midterm elections next month.
The US president’s plan had only been unveiled on Friday. He said it was the result of “a world problem” – spiralling diesel prices – which he blamed on Volodymyr Zelenskyy’s attacks on Russian oil refineries. He was going to keep the political temperature down on his war of choice in Iran and its spillover in Yemen until after the midterms.
In pursuit of this strategy, he claimed the Iranian blockade in the strait of Hormuz was in effect broken, producing figures on daily oil barrel exports to assuage the markets. As a further insurance he also claimed it was impossible to negotiate with the Iranians since there was no single leadership in Tehran.
Saudi Arabia, in conjunction with Turkey, and Pakistan, were largely to be left to sort out the Houthis along the Red Sea. The fate of Yemen, the poorest country in the world according to the International Monetary Fund, was not going to shift votes in Kansas or Alaska.
Now, after the Riyadh airport attack, it is doubtful, for a basket of reasons, that this anyway threadbare strategy, largely born of desperation, can hold.
For all the talk of the Gulf becoming responsible for its own security, it is doubtful that Saudi Arabia can be left to fend for itself after the carnage in Riyadh. One of the the 12 dead is an American and there may be more among the severely injured. Something is clearly wrong with Saudi defences since the Houthis are firing rockets from 500 miles away, and they are reaching their target too often.
The US has had a unilateral ceasefire with the Houthis since 2025, and once the hostilities between Saudi Arabia and the Houthis recommenced in the summer, the pact was quietly renewed. Trump has twice rejected Saudi appeals for help. Can he spurn them three times? The pressure to act as allies are expected to may prove overwhelming.
But “the blame Zelenskyy, trade with Russia” strategy is anyway unlikely to work. The amount of additional diesel Vladimir Putin will put on the market is not enough to bring down prices at the pump. Russia accounted for about 12% of global diesel shipped by sea. Exports have fallen by about a third, according to calculations by the Center for Research on Energy and Clean Air.
The deal has also reawakened Europe’s worst fears about Trump’s relationship with Russia only weeks after Congress had passed a sanctions package against Moscow authorising Trump to penalise buyers of Russian energy.
Europeans know full well that the inflation problem helping to drive populism is caused by the Iran war and not by Zelenskyy’s tactics. The only question is who will say so. The former UK Conservative prime minister Boris Johnson did not hold back, writing: “It is sickening that the USA is now lifting sanctions on Russian fuel, and transparently ridiculous to blame Ukraine for the spike in prices that obviously began with the closure of the strait of Hormuz. Putin is murdering innocent Ukrainian civilians every day. The Ukrainians are fighting for their lives. And yet the White House is apparently determined to force American drivers to bankroll Putin’s slaughter.”
The German education minister and a member of the CDU, Karin Prien, said: “For a few litres of diesel. What moral bankruptcy.”
Mark Rutte, the Nato secretary general and Trump ally, will this week travel to Estonia, one of the countries most supportive of Ukraine. He will have some explaining to do. Estonia’s foreign minister, Margus Tsahkna, vowed that EU foreign ministers on Monday will impose the biggest package of sanctions in its history, and he, along with Sweden, now wants to revisit using frozen Russian assets to help Ukraine.
What is worse, it looks as if the political damage to the Republicans caused by the Iran war is irrecoverable. Polls show the ship has sailed. The war has already been weaponised as a pocketbook issue by every Democratic candidate in every TV debate. Republican candidates, challenged to disown the war, are scrambling for the relative safety of identity politics.
The Iranians are also trying to maximise the damage they cause in the strait of Hormuz, countering the Trump narrative that the blockade has been broken. Scenes of tankers on fire are proliferating.
So Trump post-Riyadh needs to rethink. He can rejoin the war against the Houthis, ending the unilateral US ceasefire, but, if so, he knows from experience this will be costly. The first three weeks of the war against Iran cost $1bn a day. Many of the US president’s advisers say he may as well also attack Iran, the Houthis’ backers, as Tehran expects him to do. The Iranian military nevertheless still believes it has escalation dominance, and what happened in Riyadh can be repeated across the Gulf.
The alternative is that his team can negotiate discreetly and indirectly with Tehran as is occurring in Doha. Some Iranians are urging their leadership to seize the chance, arguing Trump is uniquely vulnerable in the run-up to the midterms.
A deal could be done, including on uranium enrichment, but it would not be palatable for the US – or for the Israeli prime minister, Benjamin Netanyahu’s re-election chances.
China and Russia are trying to sort out the Houthis diplomatically, but the Houthi objectives are broadening, while the south of Yemen is split about its future inside the country, a division that harms the country’s ability to be the ground component that Saudi Arabia needs to supplement its air-power dominance. Taiz, the third city of Yemen, is struggling to overcome a Houthi siege. The Saudis can deliver retribution, but retribution is not a solution to a decade-long civil war.
So for the moment the twin Trump strategy – to park the Iran and Yemen conflicts while blaming Zelenskyy – is now so transparent, so morally craven, and in its own terms so ineffective, that it looks unsustainable. The only question is whether the strategy will be jettisoned by the president or instead just rejected by the American people at the ballot box.

As attacks continue to mount on shipping vessels transiting the Strait of Hormuz, a seafarer's union official says some crews are being offered big rewards for making the crossing.
JOEL ROSE, HOST: President Trump blames the Russia-Ukraine war for soaring energy costs. The war with Iran, though, is the dominant factor keeping global diesel prices high. This weekend, the U.K. Maritime Trade Operations Centre reported two oil tankers traversing the Strait of Hormuz were attacked. Nearly a hundred vessels have been hit since the U.S. war with Iran began, resulting in two dozen deaths, according to the U.N. And the attacks are taking on new forms. MANOJ YADAV: Small boats that surrounded a few of the vessels and started firing with RPG and even with the guns. ROSE: Manoj Yadav is the general secretary of a union representing merchant sailors from India - many of whom work in the Persian Gulf. He says because of that danger, ship captains and sailors making these transits are being offered big money. YADAV: Not only one time, two time, three times - five times of their salary they are getting just to transit the Hormuz Strait. ROSE: Five times their regular salary. YADAV: Yeah. ROSE: And if they don't comply, Yadav says they might be punished. YADAV: And maybe there are possibility that they will get blacklisted. ROSE: Now, oil deliveries through the Strait of Hormuz have recently seen a recovery, maybe as much as 80% of the pre-war supply, according to trade intelligence firm Kpler. But the cost of getting through the choke point continues to hit pocketbooks around the world. And in the U.S., that's raising the political stakes ahead of the midterm elections.

https://english.aawsat.com/business/5328610-us-energy-secretary-oil-flows-through-hormuz-reach-80-90-pre-conflict-levels
US Energy Secretary: Oil Flows Through Hormuz Reach 80-90% of Pre-Conflict Levels
Wright to WPC Energy Congress: Refined product flows still at 25-35%
US Energy Secretary Chris Wright participating via video call in the WPC Energy Congress. (Asharq Al-Awsat)
11:01-12 October 2026 AD ـ 02 Jumada Al-Ula 1448 AH
11:01-12 October 2026 AD ـ 02 Jumada Al-Ula 1448 AH
US Energy Secretary: Oil Flows Through Hormuz Reach 80-90% of Pre-Conflict Levels
US Energy Secretary Chris Wright participating via video call in the WPC Energy Congress. (Asharq Al-Awsat)
US Energy Secretary Chris Wright said oil flows through the Strait of Hormuz had recovered to 80% to 90% of pre-conflict levels, averaging 13 million to 14 million barrels per day, while increased flows through alternative pipelines had helped support global supplies.
Speaking via video link at the WPC Energy Congress, Wright said spare production capacity and substantial inventories had helped keep crude oil supplies at manageable levels despite ongoing challenges. The crude oil supply problem was largely under control, but not fully resolved, he said.
The bigger challenge was refined petroleum product flows out of the Arabian Gulf, which remained at just 25% to 35% of pre-conflict levels, worsening shortages of diesel, jet fuel and gasoline.
Wright said the decline in Gulf refined product exports coincided with lower supplies from other sources. Russia, once a major diesel supplier, had become a net importer, albeit in limited volumes.
China had sharply cut refined product exports and announced it would not export such products in October, adding to pressure on global markets, he said.
Wright expressed hope that increased output from Chinese refineries, which have spare capacity and are benefiting from attractive profit margins, would help ease shortages. US refineries were also processing crude and producing refined products at record levels, he added.
He said the course of the Russia-Ukraine war and China's policy on refined product exports would affect supply in the coming period.
Wright said maritime shipping was another factor complicating the recovery in refined product flows from the Gulf, citing higher transport costs and longer shipping routes.
Houthi disruptions in the Bab al-Mandab Strait had forced oil tankers to take longer routes, while shifts in supply sources and Iranian attacks on vessels had added pressure on available shipping capacity, he said.
A decline in Iranian, Venezuelan and Russian oil flows through channels that had used the so-called “dark fleet” had freed up a significant number of tankers previously used to transport those supplies, Wright added.
Some of those vessels needed to be reintegrated into the mainstream shipping system, with sanctions lifted where possible, to increase capacity for transporting refined products from the Gulf and support continued oil and gas operations, he said.
Wright warned that higher shipping costs were having a greater impact on markets farther from supply sources. Global diesel prices had reached very high levels, while jet fuel prices had also risen, alongside a more moderate increase in gasoline prices.
Spare production capacity and inventories had helped contain pressure on the crude oil market, he said, but shortages of refined products and constraints on maritime shipping continued to pose challenges.
Wright concluded that the response to the conflict had demonstrated the energy system's ability to adapt, but stressed that the crisis was not over and that continued efforts were needed to maintain stable supplies.

Miras: Energy Security, Sustainability, or Affordability Cannot Be Sacrificed

10:21-12 October 2026 AD ـ 02 Jumada Al-Ula 1448 AH

Miras: Energy Security, Sustainability, or Affordability Cannot Be Sacrificed
Pedro Miras addresses attendees as the 25th WPC kicks off in Riyadh (Asharq Al-Awsat)
Pedro Miras, President of WPC Energy, affirmed that the world needs more energy from multiple sources to keep pace with population and economic growth, emphasizing the necessity of balancing supply security, affordability, sustainability, and economic development, without sacrificing any of these priorities.
In his speech at the opening of the 25th WPC in Riyadh, Miras stated that Riyadh is the most suitable place to host the conference, noting the pivotal role Saudi Arabia plays in the global energy system, and its continued leadership in investments, innovation, and technological advancement in the sector.
He added that the conference, which gathers government and industry leaders, academics, technology experts, financiers, and international institutions over four days, serves as a platform to discuss ways to meet the world's increasing energy needs, while supporting prosperity, sustainability, and security for future generations.
He pointed out that the world is undergoing a period of profound transformations, significant challenges, and exceptional opportunities, with the global population expected to approach 10 billion by mid-century, accompanied by increased energy demand resulting from population growth, urbanization, and improved living standards.
He explained that energy consumption and human development have gone hand-in-hand throughout modern history, as the availability of energy has contributed to improving health, education, and living standards, emphasizing that continued human progress requires more energy.
He noted that oil and gas still provide a significant share of the world's energy needs, despite decades of innovation and growth in new technologies, considering this an affirmation of the scale of the upcoming challenge and the need to utilize multiple energy sources.
Miras stressed that the challenge is no longer about choosing between energy security, affordability, and sustainability, but rather about achieving progress in these areas in parallel with economic development.
He stated that billions of people around the world aspire to improve their living conditions, access better health and education services, and broader economic opportunities, all of which require reliable energy availability and continued investment in its system.
He added that countries differ in their circumstances, resources, priorities, and developmental stages, which necessitates adopting diverse paths to achieve the future of energy and utilizing a wide range of technologies and solutions, noting that this diversity represents a source of strength.
He affirmed that technological neutrality remains one of the fundamental principles of the WPC, allowing various energy sources and technologies to contribute to building a secure, affordable, and sustainable system.
Innovation Alone Is Not Enough Without Investment and Partnerships
Regarding technological developments, Miras said that artificial intelligence, digitalization, advanced materials, new fuels, hydrogen, and carbon capture, utilization, and storage technologies, along with other technologies, are reshaping the global energy landscape.
However, he stressed that technology alone is not enough to achieve the desired transformation, as it requires effective policies, stable and predictable regulatory frameworks, long-term investments, and enhanced international cooperation.
He explained that energy security remains essential for economic growth, social stability, and human well-being, and that a successful energy sector transformation cannot be achieved without ensuring supply security. Furthermore, the long-term sustainability of this security requires sufficient investments across various components of the energy system.
He added that no single country, company, or technology can address global energy challenges alone, emphasizing the importance of partnerships among governments, industry, academia, and international institutions.
Miras expressed his appreciation to the Kingdom, the Minister of Energy, and the Saudi Minister of Industry and Mineral Resources, Prince Abdulaziz bin Salman, for their efforts in organizing and hosting the conference in Riyadh.
He stated that for over 90 years, the WPC has provided a platform bringing together governments, industry, academia, and international institutions to exchange ideas, deepen understanding, and explore practical solutions for sector challenges.
He noted that the importance of dialogue and cooperation is increasing amid the uncertainty and fragmentation the world is experiencing, expressing his hope that the conference discussions will contribute to developing partnerships and exploring innovations that will shape the future of energy.
He concluded by affirming that the goal is to build an energy future that leaves no one behind, achieving progress in supply security, affordability, sustainability, and economic development, based on innovation, cooperation, and mutual respect.

Saudi Energy Minister: No Room for Fear in Our Vocabulary … No Wasted Opportunities to Realize Our Vision
Saudi Energy Minister Prince Abdulaziz bin Salman speaks at the opening of the WPC Energy Congress (Asharq Al-Awsat)
Saudi Energy Minister Prince Abdulaziz bin Salman said there was “no room for fear in the vocabulary of Saudis,” stressing that the resilience of the kingdom’s energy system and the determination of its people strengthened its ability to weather crises, ensure uninterrupted supplies and continue its transformation drive toward the goals of Vision 2030.
He also highlighted the importance of long-term investment in infrastructure, developing supply chains and localizing industries to help Saudi Arabia navigate global challenges and changes while preserving opportunities for future generations in a more sustainable future.
Prince Abdulaziz made the remarks in his opening address at the 25th WPC Energy Congress, held as part of Riyadh Energy Week on Monday, in the presence of a large number of ministers, officials and industry leaders.
He said recent experiences had demonstrated the importance of advance preparedness and building an energy system capable of adapting to different circumstances. Resilience, he added, was measured not only by a system’s ability to withstand disruptions, but also by its capacity to repair damage, restore operations and continue meeting market needs.
Prince Abdulaziz stressed that energy system resilience and reliable supplies were fundamental to addressing global challenges and changing conditions. He said the kingdom would continue investing in energy infrastructure, developing supply chains and strengthening human capabilities and modern technologies to ensure uninterrupted supplies under all circumstances.
He said the conference was being held at a pivotal moment for the global energy sector, adding that Saudi Arabia’s hosting of participants in its capital reflected the kingdom’s commitment to providing a safe environment and that the safety of its guests was a top priority.
The meeting, he said, embodied a commitment to prudent decision-making, practical cooperation and a shared sense of responsibility within the energy community, as the sector faces mounting challenges related to energy security and supply stability that are testing the resilience and adaptability of countries and economic systems.
Prince Abdulaziz said Saudi Arabia had invested over the years in developing infrastructure, improving supply chain efficiency and building human capabilities to ensure the energy system was prepared to cope with exceptional circumstances.
He cited the East-West oil pipeline as a key example of infrastructure that provides alternative routes for transporting oil to markets, strengthening the kingdom’s ability to manage risks that could disrupt traditional supply routes.
These capabilities, he said, had not been developed overnight, but were the result of years of planning, development and operational experience, supported by Saudi Aramco’s technical and operational expertise, industrial capabilities and transportation and supply networks, which help ensure business continuity under varying conditions.
Prince Abdulaziz bin Salman stressed the importance of localizing supply chains and increasing local content, describing them as strategic objectives for Saudi Arabia. He cited Saudi Aramco’s announcement that it had achieved a 70% local content rate, a milestone reflecting the growing capabilities of national industries and their role in strengthening the sector’s resilience.
The minister said the value of these investments becomes more apparent when global supply chains are disrupted, as advanced systems and domestic capabilities help address gaps and mitigate the impact of interruptions, supporting energy system stability and ensuring continued operations.
He said resilience depends on skilled personnel, technical expertise and rapid response, enabling critical facilities to recover from damage and maintain services.
The energy minister reaffirmed the kingdom’s commitment to continued investment across the energy sector, developing new technologies and reducing emissions, while also investing in national talent and preparing future generations.
Prince Abdulaziz bin Salman said Saudi Arabia aims to build an energy system capable of sustained success and adapting to changing circumstances while safeguarding the security and sustainability of supplies.
He stressed that the kingdom has the resources needed to sustain economic growth and pursue its development vision, supporting the prosperity of its people and strengthening international partnerships.
While no one can predict when current conditions will end, recent experience underscores the importance of preparing for the future, developing solutions before they are needed and strengthening the ability to respond to rapidly changing demands, he said.
Prince Abdulaziz called for Riyadh to serve as a platform for deeper cooperation and translating international partnerships into practical action, with discussions focused on meeting global energy needs, reducing emissions and strengthening resilience to crises and challenges.
He said the world needed reliable partners and leaders willing to make long-term strategic decisions, reaffirming that Saudi Arabia would continue to play its role in the energy sector through an approach based on prudence, openness and expanding international partnerships.
He concluded by welcoming participants to Riyadh and calling for joint efforts to build a secure and resilient energy future that ensures reliable supplies and meets the needs of societies and economies worldwide.

### 11. ★★★☆☆ Fed Chair Kevin Warsh and the FOMC Just Fired a Warning Shot at the Second-Priciest Stock Market in History - The Motley Fool

- **分类**: 📈 市场
- **来源**: GN Fed / GN Markets
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-12T08:40:06+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Fed Chair Kevin Warsh and the FOMC Just Fired a Warning Shot at the Second-Priciest Stock Market in History&nbsp;&nbsp;The Motl

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

This has been a year highlighted by a changing of the guard. Longtime CEOs Warren Buffett and Tim Cook handed over the keys to their respective successors, and President Donald Trump&#x27;s Fed chair nominee, Kevin Warsh, became only the 17th head of the central bank on May 22.
Despite these changes, Wall Street&#x27;s preeminent stock indexes, the Dow Jones Industrial Average ( ^DJI +0.83% ) , S&P 500 ( ^GSPC +0.59% ) , and Nasdaq Composite ( ^IXIC +0.64% ) have been unstoppable. The artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure build-out and otherworldly growth prospects accompanying this game-changing innovation have lifted the broader market to new heights.
This won&#x27;t be a one-and-done rate-hiking cycle. Image source: Official Federal Reserve Photo.
But sustaining this AI-driven bull market could be easier said than done. Last week, Fed Chair Warsh and his Federal Open Market Committee (FOMC) colleagues fired an unmistakable warning shot at the second-priciest stock market in history. The only question is: How many investors are paying attention?
The Federal Reserve&#x27;s rate-hiking cycle is ramping up
On Sept. 16, Warsh and 11 other voting members of the FOMC unanimously raised the federal funds target rate by 25 basis points to 3.75%-4.00%. It&#x27;s just the fourth time this century that the central bank has kicked off a rate-hiking cycle .
Warsh and the FOMC have been compelled to act because of persistently elevated inflation. August marked the 66th consecutive month in which the annualized inflation rate topped the FOMC&#x27;s long-term 2% target.
"The plain fact is that inflation is too high and has been for too long," https://t.co/htRQqyQHtr
While rising prices are perfectly normal in an expanding economy, inflation has soared in 2026 due to Trumpflation (inflation that&#x27;s specifically driven by President Trump&#x27;s policies). Donald Trump&#x27;s tariffs are increasing domestic manufacturing costs, while the Iran war has led to the ongoing closure of the Strait of Hormuz. The largest energy supply disruption in modern history has sent fuel prices soaring.
The newly released Federal Reserve meeting minutes (effectively a summary of what policymakers discussed behind closed doors on Sept. 15-16) point to tariffs, the Iran war, and the AI infrastructure build-out as key sources of inflation.
More importantly, the Fed meeting minutes charted a path forward for interest rates. Contained in the Fed minutes was this statement:
With regard to the outlook for monetary policy beyond this current meeting, most participants assessed that another increase in the target range for the federal funds rate would likely be appropriate by year end. While this statement doesn&#x27;t tell us whether the FOMC would raise interest rates at the October or December meeting, it makes it crystal clear that additional rate hikes are expected in the not-too-distant future. That&#x27;s terrible news for a historically expensive stock market.
Stock Market Shiller PE Ratio on the verge of taking out its Dot Com Bubble all-time high 🚨 🤯 👀 https://t.co/CtCmSgWnLt
A truly jaw-dropping amount of capital is being spent on the AI data center build-out , part of which has been financed with debt. If lending costs rise, as the FOMC&#x27;s September meeting minutes imply, it could slow this expansion and adversely impact lofty growth expectations.
In June, the S&P 500&#x27;s Shiller Price-to-Earnings Ratio came within a stone&#x27;s throw of surpassing the priciest valuation multiple in 156 years , achieved just before the dot-com bubble burst. This is a stock market with little to no margin for error. If the Fed&#x27;s rate-hiking cycle ramps up, maintaining this aggressive AI build-out without any "hiccups" would likely prove impossible.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

President Donald Trump said that Russia and Ukraine will not be targeting each other&#x27;s energy facilities.
Al Jazeera reported that Iran was ready to provide assistance to help with the start of dialogue between Saudi Arabia and Yemen&#x27;s Houthis.
Oil prices are likely to remain volatile in the near term amid concerns over the Middle East conflict and the impact of Hurricane Isaias.
This photo shows a general view of fuel tanks at the fuel depot near King Khalid International Airport in Riyadh on Sept. 19, 2026.
Oil prices reversed initial losses early Monday, despite the potential for improved supplies after U.S. President Donald Trump declared an " energy ceasefire" between Russia and Ukraine.
Futures for international benchmark Brent crude for December delivery rose to $106.17 a barrel. U.S.  West Texas Intermediate futures  for November ticked 0.54% higher to $92.76 per barrel. Prices on both benchmarks had fallen by around 1.5% earlier in the session.
"Effective immediately, there is an ENERGY CEASEFIRE in the War between Russia and Ukraine. Both parties have agreed. Do not break it!" Trump said in a social media statement Sunday stateside.
Oil reversed its losses after Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelenskyy welcomed such a ceasefire, but warned Monday that Moscow must do the same.
Prospects of more Russian energy hitting markets after a deal with the U.S. have also risen after Trump said Friday that Moscow will supply more than 4 million tons of diesel to the global market under a deal he struck with Putin during a phone call.
According to the Saudi state news agency , Iran&#x27;s Minister of Foreign Affairs Abbas Araghchi made a phone call to Saudi Arabia&#x27;s Minister of Foreign Affairs Faisal bin Farhan bin Abdullah to discuss the region&#x27;s recent escalation of events as well as the efforts to address it.
Al Jazeera reported Araghchi had said Iran was ready to provide assistance to help with the start of dialogue between Saudi Arabia and Yemen&#x27;s Houthis.
Oil prices are expected to remain volatile in the near term as the U.S.-Iran conflict continues, while the impact of Hurricane Isaias on U.S. energy producers could also hurt supplies .

<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Iran’s Revolutionary Guard Navy says oil tanker struck mine in Strait of Hormuz
Iran’s Revolutionary Guard Navy says oil tanker struck mine in Strait of Hormuz
IRGC Navy says explosion struck engine room of supertanker capable of carrying 2 million barrels of oil
ISTANBUL An oil tanker struck a mine while attempting to cross the Strait of Hormuz, triggering an explosion in its engine room, Iran’s Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps (IRGC) Navy said Sunday.
The IRGC Navy said in a statement that the supertanker, capable of carrying around 2 million barrels of oil, was attempting to transit the strategic waterway with its navigation system switched off.
The oil tanker is the third vessel to strike naval mines along the prohibited route in the Strait of Hormuz since Saturday, it added.
The statement did not identify the vessel or specify its nationality or flag.
The IRGC Navy renewed its warning that vessels switching off their navigation systems while passing through the waterway would be classified as “hostile vessels.”
The announcement came after the US Central Command (CENTCOM) said in late August that international shipping lanes in the Strait of Hormuz had been reopened following the removal of Iranian naval mines by US forces.
Iran announced on July 12 that it had again closed the strait following the resumption of US attacks on July 8.
Tehran has threatened to target vessels using routes other than those designated by Iranian authorities or attempting to cross the waterway without permission.
The US and Israel launched a war against Iran on Feb. 28. Tehran responded with missile and drone attacks across the region and effectively closed the Strait of Hormuz, while Washington later imposed a naval blockade on Iranian ports.
The US and Iran subsequently signed a memorandum of understanding in June concerning freedom of navigation in the strait, but implementation stalled over disagreements related to security in the strategic waterway.

### 14. ★★★☆☆ Netanyahu demands probe into sources for award-winning Gaza film NAZA

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: Al Jazeera
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-12T04:24:18+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Documentary draws on 24 anonymous Israeli sources accusing military of killing Palestinians in Gaza using AI systems.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Share Netanyahu demands probe into sources for award-winning Gaza film NAZA on social media
A man walks past an image of directors Rachel Szor and Yuval Abraham, winners of the special jury prize at the Venice International Film Festival for their film NAZA, with the Hebrew word for "traitors" scrawled across in graffiti, in Ashdod, Israel, on September 15, 2026 [Ariel Schalit/AP]
Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu has called for an investigation into the anonymous sources featured in a documentary alleging that Israel’s military killed civilians in Gaza using AI-powered targeting systems.
The award-winning film, NAZA, draws on testimony from 24 unnamed Israeli military whistleblowers accusing the army of using the systems to kill numerous civilians in the besieged territory.
list of 3 items list 1 of 3 Child among eight killed by Israel on Gaza ‘ceasefire’ anniversary
list 2 of 3 Hundreds of doctors demand World Medical Association suspend Israel
list 3 of 3 How genocidal war on Gaza is shaping Israel’s election
“I demand that the chief of general staff, the head of the Shin Bet, and the attorney general launch a comprehensive investigation into the participants in the film ‘Naza’ and prosecute them for serious information security violations, criminal libel, and harm to Israel’s diplomatic and security interests,” Netanyahu said on X on Saturday evening.
“The film presents a false, misleading, and biased narrative that defames the State of Israel and the [Israeli military] internationally, severely damaging Israel’s standing,” he added.
In a statement last month, the Israeli military said it “categorically” rejected the whistleblowers’ allegations, calling some of their assertions “absurd’ and contradictory.
It insisted that the selection and approval of strikes “are made by human personnel only”, in line with the military’s guidelines.
Israeli army Chief of Staff Eyal Zamir has called for legal action against the participants to be considered and ordered an investigation into suspected leaks of classified military information used in the film’s production.
NAZA takes its name from the Israeli military term for civilian casualties expected in air strikes, an abbreviated form of the Hebrew phrase for “collateral damage”.
The film was awarded a special jury prize at the Venice International Film Festival and received a historic 25-minute standing ovation.
The film’s directors, Yuval Abraham and Rachel Szor, have been accused of “treason”. Netanyahu and Culture Minister Miki Zohar have threatened to revoke their Israeli citizenship.
Last week, the head of Israel’s National Public Diplomacy Directorate, Tzipi Hotovely, awarded the creators of another film, NAZA: The Real Story, certificates of recognition for countering the “blood libels” made in the original NAZA film.
According to a statement from the Prime Minister’s Office, NAZA: The Real Story features testimony from soldiers refuting the original claims.
In contrast, 43 Israeli rights groups defended the filmmakers of NAZA and denounced the threats being directed at them, according to Israel’s Channel 12 news.
Israel’s genocidal war on Gaza began on October 7, 2023, after Hamas’s armed wing, the Qassam Brigades, and other Palestinian groups attacked southern Israel, killing 1,139 people and taking about 240 captive.
Since then, Israel has killed at least 74,000 Palestinians, including at least 21,000 children, according to Gaza’s Ministry of Health.

Natural Gas and Oil Forecast: WTI Tests $91.40, Can Brent Break $106 and NG Reach $3.35?
Gulf of Mexico oil production resumes after Hurricane Isaias shut in 1.5 million barrels per day, while U.S. crude inventories fell 3.2 million barrels to 424.1 million barrels.
WTI crude oil consolidates near $91.40 within a tightening triangle, with a breakout targeting $93.54 and $96.05, while $88.77 remains critical support.
Brent crude oil breaks above its descending channel and holds $102.64, strengthening the bullish outlook toward $106.03 and potentially $109.79 despite uncertainty over Russian supply.
Natural gas rebounds from $3.11 and holds above its moving averages, with a breakout above $3.24 potentially exposing $3.35 and $3.44, while $3.02 remains the next downside target.
USOIL: Gulf Output Returns as Russian Supply Prospects Improve
U.S. crude fundamentals have changed following Hurricane Isaias’ disruption of Gulf of Mexico (GOM) production. At the storm’s peak, about 1.5 million barrels a day (b/d) or 72% of Gulf of Mexico crude production was shut-in. Of the major producers, Shell, Chevron and BP have started returning workers and have restarted production.
According to the EIA , commercial crude oil inventories for the week ended October 2, decreased by 3.2 million barrels to 424.1 million barrels, and exports averaged 4.77 million b/d, up about 1.2 million b/d from the prior week. Refinery utilization rate was at 92.7%.
Next up Natural Gas News: Weekly Forecast Sees Warm Weather Capping Gas Rally
UKOil: Russia Ceasefire Hopes Meet Persistent Hormuz Risk
Elsewhere on the international front, the supply balance shifted after U.S. President Donald Trump announced what he called an energy ceasefire between Russia and Ukraine, and that Russia would immediately supply more than 300,000 metric tons of diesel , followed by 500,000 metric tons in November. Neither Moscow nor Kyiv confirmed the deal.
Middle East risk remains high. Iran says that the U.S. response to its conditions for re-opening the Strait of Hormuz was unsatisfactory. Before the war, roughly 20% of the world’s oil and LNG shipments transited the Strait. Approximately 2.3 million barrels per day of Middle Eastern refining capacity remains offline adding to the global fuel supply deficit.
Natural Gas: U.S. Storage Improves as Qatar LNG Remains Constrained
U.S. working gas inventories increased by 85 billion cubic feet (Bcf) to 3,500 Bcf. Gas inventories are 2% higher than the five-year average for this time of the year.
Global LNG supplies remain constrained. Around 17% of Qatar’s export capacity is offline. September LNG shipments recovered to 1.6 million tons, still less than a quarter of the pre-war average. So far in October, LNG shipments remain tracked at 0.6 million tons.

Natural Gas Technical Analysis: NG Holds $3.11 as $3.24 Becomes the Next Upside Test
Natural gas is currently trading at around $3.18 on the 2 hour chart after bouncing up from $3.11. What interests me is price currently trading above both moving averages and the overall trend of higher lows is still valid despite the most recent pullback from the $3.30 area.
I see $3.24 as the first resistance area and beyond that $3.35, $3.44, and so on. $3.11 is support again, and if that breaks, focus shifts to $3.02, $2.92, $2.82, and so on.
RSI is back to the neutral zone after being overbought which means that price has just pulled back from being overbought. I like the long side as long as price trades above $3.11. I am also interested in the $3.24 area to the upside, where a break above opens the door to $3.35. If $3.11 breaks to the downside, $3.02 opens.
WTI Crude Oil Technical Analysis: USOIL Tests $91.40 as Triangle Compression Builds
WTI crude oil is currently trading at $91.31. What is important about this price action is the compression between the descending trendline and the rising support line. The moving averages are also starting to flatten. This is short term compressing action.
The first area of interest is $91.40. A break above that would target $93.54, then $96.05, and $98.08. Support comes in at $88.77, then $86.89 and $85.24.
RSI is showing a neutral picture, but, like WTI, is caught in the middle of the range. A break above $91.40 would be bullish and a breakdown below $88.77 would put the focus back on the $86.89 level.
Brent Crude Oil Technical Analysis: UKOIL Breaks Higher as $106.03 Comes Into Focus
Brent Crude is currently trading at $104.59 with bullish momentum breaking through the upper trendline of the descending channel. Price is currently above the $102.64 area and both moving averages. This is a positive short-term development compared to WTI.
The price of Brent is now above $102.64 and both moving averages. This creates an even stronger case than WTI. I am looking for $106.03 for the first target. $102.64 is the first support and below that $98.71, $95.64, and $93.15 are areas of interest.
RSI is showing positive momentum and bullish condition. As long as $102.64 does not break down, the bias should be bullish, especially as long as Brent trades above the channel. A break above $106.03 supports the case for $109.79 and a break below $102.64 invalidates the bullish development.
