<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

VW is exhibiting at the Paris show for the first time in 20 years, unveiling its new all-electric ID Tiguan. Photograph: Volkswagen AG
VW is exhibiting at the Paris show for the first time in 20 years, unveiling its new all-electric ID Tiguan. Photograph: Volkswagen AG
Volkswagen boss backs EU plan to bolster domestic European industry
Oliver Blume says ‘Made in Europe’ rules to counter rapid rise of lower-price Chinese brands ‘must reward real value creation’
The boss of Volkswagen has backed EU proposals to boost its domestic industry, arguing European carmakers need to be able to “compete under comparable conditions” with Chinese rivals.
Oliver Blume said “Made in Europe” rules from the EU “must reward real value creation in Europe”, speaking in Paris before the motor show this week.
The rules are aimed at countering the rapid rise of Chinese brands in Europe in the automotive sector and other important manufacturing industries. Lower-priced Chinese manufacturers have prompted a crisis in several industries.
Volkswagen is among the most-affected European companies. Germany’s biggest carmaker has launched a brutal plan to cut as many as 100,000 jobs . Blume said it was the “largest transformation programme in its history”.
Alarm bells sound in Brussels as EU sales of Chinese hybrid cars rocket
The proposals for the Made in Europe rules, formally known as the Industrial Accelerator Act, would limit subsidies and public procurement to products with a large proportion of their materials and manufacturing coming from within the EU.
Blume listed a litany of challenges facing European carmakers, saying: “Europe is under enormous competitive pressure.” These included: high energy costs; lower demand as consumers struggle with high inflation; the need to be quicker to develop cars; and intense competition from China .
On Friday, the EU said it had reached a landmark deal with China to “halve” its sales of hybrid cars in the bloc amid fears surging sales could kill off parts of the European car industry.
Blume said Germany’s carmakers were happy to compete with Chinese rivals, but said the EU should try to reward businesses with significant European footprints.
“Companies that invest and develop in Europe must see a clear benefit,” he said.
Referring to Chinese companies, he added: “Those who sell here should compete under comparable conditions and create jobs and value here in Europe, too.
The new all-electric ID Tiguan. Photograph: Volkswagen AG Blume added: “We believe in open markets, but openness also means standing up for our own interests in Europe. Because a strong industrial base means more than economic success. It protects technological independence.”
Volkswagen is exhibiting at the Paris show for the first time in 20 years. Blume emphasised the “close ties between France and Germany”, while hosting the French industry minister, Sébastian Martin.
Arno Antlitz, VW’s chief financial officer, said the job cuts were necessary to “increase our economic power” by increasing profitability and allowing it to defend its “home turf”.
The company is cutting the number of models it offers worldwide from 150 to 75 across brands including Audi and Škoda. It hopes the move will reduce complexity in its factories and for dealers, cutting the cost of each vehicle it makes.
On Sunday, it revealed the production version of its ID Tiguan, an electric edition of its bestselling family SUV.

Oil markets have become a live stress test for portfolios, with Brent around $103, a strong US dollar, and bond yields resetting borrowing costs all at once. Geopolitical risk in the Middle East and upcoming US CPI data keep energy producers on the front line of every macro headline. This article walks through 3 stocks from our Global Integrated Oil & Gas and Upstream Energy Producers screener that look most exposed to these shocks, and explains why that might matter for your next move.
The stocks covered below are only a small sample, and the full screen surfaced 22 more large oil and gas and upstream producers with equally compelling stories that are not unpacked in this article. To go straight to the source and identify your own highest conviction ideas, analyze the Global Integrated Oil & Gas and Upstream Energy Producers screener .
Kodiak Gas Services plugs straight into the screener theme as a large US energy infrastructure player, keeping gas and oil flowing for upstream producers that rely on compression capacity rather than owning it outright.
Kodiak Gas Services runs compressor fleets and related infrastructure for US oil and gas producers under long term contracts, with about US$1.22b of revenue from compression infrastructure and roughly US$139 million from other services, and carries a market value near US$5.41b.
"Rising natural gas demand and expanding Permian Basin production, supported by incremental pipeline takeaway projects and ongoing shifts to deeper, gassier zones, are still expected to sustain high utilization of Kodiak Gas Services’ large horsepower compression fleet and support revenue growth from contract services."
What happens to Kodiak margins and cash generation if a single cost pressure quietly eats into that contracted compression story?
If that quiet cost squeeze is what worries you, read the full narrative for Kodiak Gas Services to see how Kodiak Gas Services’ contracts, fleet scale and balance sheet could be masking upside.
NYSE:KGS Revenue & Expenses Breakdown as at Oct 2026 NOV (NOV)
NOV sits in this screener as a large supplier of drilling and production kit that directly feeds the capital spending cycles of integrated oil majors and upstream producers when oil markets heat up.
NOV Inc. supplies drilling and production equipment and services that link directly into upstream oil and gas activity, with Energy Equipment generating about US$5.0b of revenue and Energy Products and Services around US$3.8b, and the stock valued near US$6.8b.
"Deepwater projects that use floating production systems and subsea kit continue to build multi year order books for NOV, with record EBITDA margins in Energy Equipment, a book to bill of 135% and a 28% backlog rise in subsea flexible pipe, and an approved US$200 million Brazil plant expansion."
What investors really need to watch is how one less visible pressure shapes the translation of that backlog into cash and sustainable margins.
That quiet pressure point is exactly what the full narrative for NOV unpacks, showing where NOV’s backlog strength could be masking accelerating or stalled cash generation.
NYSE:NOV Revenue & Expenses Breakdown as at Oct 2026 Helmerich & Payne (HP)
Helmerich & Payne is one of the purest ways in this screener to tap into the global drilling cycle, with its rigs and digital tools closely tied to how aggressively producers respond when crude prices move and security of supply becomes a top priority.
Helmerich & Payne runs drilling solutions across North America, offshore and international markets, generating about US$2.22b from North America Solutions, US$944 million from International Solutions and US$714 million from Offshore Solutions, with a market value around US$4.0b.
"Continued digitalization and automation of drilling operations through tools such as AutoSlide and FlexRobotics, including record well performance in Vaca Muerta, supports differentiated pricing and can contribute to higher revenue and direct margin expansion as more rigs adopt these systems."
What really decides how Helmerich & Payne converts this drilling footprint into equity returns is how one unresolved cost and conflict pressure shapes future margins.
That unresolved cost and conflict pressure is exactly what the full narrative for Helmerich & Payne pulls apart, spotlighting where Helmerich & Payne&#x27;s drilling footprint could be quietly decoupling cash generation from headline activity.

Dow Jones, S&P 500, Nasdaq 100 Outlook: Top Stock Market News This Week
The Dow Jones , S&P 500 and Nasdaq 100 have held steady in recent weeks as investors have focused on rising US bond yields and the escalating situation in the Middle East. Their resilience will be tested in the coming weeks as major US companies report earnings and key economic data is released.
Dow Jones, S&P 500, and Nasdaq 100 to React to Earnings Season
The main catalyst for the US stock market this week will be the upcoming earnings season , which will kick off on Tuesday. Top US banks, including popular names like Goldman Sachs, Morgan Stanley and JPMorgan, will release their numbers. Other non-bank companies that will release their numbers are Johnson & Johnson, UnitedHealth Group and Kinder Morgan.
Analysts are optimistic about this earnings season. FactSet (NYSE: FDS ) data shows that the estimate among analysts is that earnings growth will be 29.5%. Historically, the real earnings growth tends to be 7% above the estimate. As such, there is a likelihood that the growth will be over 40%.
The earnings growth comes at a time when American stocks are trading at discount levels. For example, the forward price-to-earnings ratio for the S&P 500 Index is 19.1, lower than the five and ten-year averages.
Why Micron Stock is Struggling Despite Revenue Growth and a Low Valuation
Micron stock price has remained inside a narrow range in the past few days despite its strong revenue growth and cheap valuation
The stock market will also react to the upcoming US consumer price index (CPI) report on Wednesday. Economists expect the data to show that the headline and core CPI numbers rose last month as gasoline and diesel prices jumped.
The average estimate among analysts is that the headline CPI rose from 3.4% in August to 3.6% in September. Core inflation, which excludes the volatile food and energy prices, is expected to come in at 2.5%, up slightly from the previous 2.4%.
These numbers will provide more hints about what to expect from the Federal Reserve. Economists expect the bank to leave rates unchanged in this meeting and then hike in December.
The stock market will also react to the bond market , where the ten-year and five-year yields have pulled back in the past few days.
The US stock market will also react to the ongoing crisis in the Middle East, which is escalating. For example, Yemen’s Houthis launched a major attack against Saudi Arabia’s main airport, with President Donald Trump hinting that the US will intervene .
Iran has also continued striking ships crossing the Strait of Hormuz. There is also fear that the crisis will escalate. If this happens, it means that crude oil prices will continue rising in the coming weeks. This, in turn, will lead to higher inflation, pushing the Fed to hike interest rates, which may affect the stock market.

Traders are leaning into positive index futures while evaluating Middle East headlines and incoming Q3 corporate earnings:
Positive Index Futures: The stock index futures indicate a green open across major benchmarks. Dow Jones futures are leading the advance, up 0.84%. S&P 500 futures rose 0.56%. Nasdaq 100 futures gained 0.45%, and Russell 2000 futures added 0.33%.
Cooling Oil Relieves Rate Pressures: Energy prices pulled back in early trading. Brent crude fell 1.27% to $103.39 a barrel, while WTI crude dropped 1.61% to $90.37 a barrel. The pullbacks offer relief following news that Iran rejected Washington’s latest response to its peace proposal as “insufficient” and threatened critical digital infrastructure across the region if fighting intensifies.
Big Bank Earnings Season Arrives: Wall Street’s focus is shifting to third-quarter corporate results. Major financial institutions—including JPMorgan Chase & Co. (NYSE: JPM ), Bank of America Corp. (NYSE: BAC ), Citigroup Inc. (NYSE: C ), Wells Fargo & Company (NYSE: WFC ), Morgan Stanley (NYSE: MS ), and Goldman Sachs Group Inc. (NYSE: GS )—are scheduled to report quarterly results this week. Investors will be watching executive commentary on consumer health and net interest income amid elevated interest rates.
Economic Calendar: The bond market is closed on Monday for Columbus Day, though the equity market remains open. Later this week, investors will digest September Consumer Price Index (CPI) inflation data, retail sales figures, and the Federal Reserve’s Beige Book.
Equities maintain strong momentum after all major stock indexes closed higher on Friday. As detailed in market commentary from Ed Yardeni , last week saw a notable shift as interest-rate-sensitive sectors led gains while Treasury yields edged down. Consumer Staples, Utilities, Consumer Discretionary, and Financials all posted gains as the rally broadened.
Market strategist Yardeni noted that Corporate America’s “Fabulous Earnings Momentum” (FEMO) continues to provide a solid foundation for stock valuations. Consensus expectations for third-quarter S&P 500 earnings growth have been revised upward to 30.6% year-over-year, with all 11 S&P 500 sectors projected to report positive revenue and profit growth simultaneously.
The Oct. 9 Polymarket contract resolved “Up”. The contract recorded $34,323 in total trading volume.

SPY rose 0.60% to $778.57, while QQQ advanced 0.49% to $751.27. Meanwhile, the Dow tracker, State Street SPDR Dow Jones Industrial Average ETF Trust (NYSE: DIA ), ended 0.87% higher at $516.11.

Real estate, consumer discretionary and health care stocks recorded the biggest gains, while communication services and energy bucked the overall market trend to close lower, as most S&P 500 sectors ended Friday positive.
In his analysis, Mohamed El-Erian characterizes the U.S. economy as fundamentally strong yet navigating an increasingly precarious environment. He notes that the economy remains robust—growing in the “3–4% range, according to the Atlanta Fed GDPNow measure”—supported by a “strong labor market.”
However, he cautions that this expansion coexists with significant fiscal headwinds, pointing out that the U.S. budget deficit reached “around 6% of GDP, despite a robust economy.”
Regarding the stock market, El-Erian observes that U.S. equity indices continue to reach “new record highs,” but warns that this growth is highly fragile, being “driven almost exclusively by narrow momentum in the technology sector.”
Looking forward, he highlights that the Federal Reserve is “navigating an increasingly narrow path” as it balances robust economic activity against inflationary pressures. While policymakers are threading a “tight needle” to maintain stability—making an October rate hike unlikely—upcoming CPI data could upend expectations.
El-Erian warns that a hotter-than-expected inflation print could trigger further fixed-income volatility and increase financial strain, as evidence already suggests “some leakage of interest-rate risk into credit risk.”
Here’s what investors will be keeping an eye on this week.
On Tuesday, September’s NFIB index of small business optimism data will be released by 6:00 a.m. ET.
September’s existing home sales data will be out by 10:00 a.m., and September’s monthly Treasury balance data will be released by 2:00 p.m. ET.
On Wednesday, September’s CPI, core CPI, annual CPI, and annual core CPI data will be released by 8:30 a.m. ET.
The Federal Reserve Beige Book will be released by 2:00 p.m. ET.
On Thursday, October’s Empire State manufacturing survey, September’s retail sales, October’s Philadelphia Fed business outlook survey, initial jobless claims for the week ending Oct. 10, September’s PPI, core PPI, and annual PPI data will all be released by 8:30 a.m. ET.
August’s manufacturing and trade inventories data will be out by 10:00 a.m. ET.
Federal Reserve Bank of Cleveland President Beth Hammack will speak at 4:30 p.m., and Fed Chairman Kevin Warsh will speak at 11:30 p.m. ET.
On Friday, September’s import prices data will be released by 8:30 a.m. ET.
September’s industrial production and capacity utilization data will be out by 9:15 a.m. ET.
Crude Oil WTI futures were trading higher in the early New York session by 0.79% to hover around $92.58 per barrel.
Gold Spot US Dollar fell 0.40% to hover around $4,177.47 per ounce. The U.S. Dollar Index spot was 0.1% higher at the 102.31 level.
Meanwhile, Bitcoin (CRYPTO: BTC) was trading 0.70% higher at $83,513.47 per coin over the last 24 hours.
Asian markets traded mixed on Monday, as Hong Kong’s Hang Seng and Australia’s S&P/ASX 200 indices rose. While China’s CSI 30, India’s Nifty 50, South Korea’s Kospi and Japan’s Nikkei 225 indices declined. European markets were mostly higher in early trade.

### 26. ★★★☆☆ BOK chief says early rate hike ahead of Fed's minimized economic costs - The Korea Times

- **分类**: 💰 全球金融
- **来源**: GN Fed
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-12T07:09:03+00:00
- **发生了什么**: <a href="https://news.google.com/rss/articles/CBMitgFBVV95cUxPSkRydzk0RHFMcUhIVDkxTlJldE9GLTFUanB5NWJLQTF0NTlwR3NDM1VLbHNTQ3JQMFJHZ25wV0Rud0FyWmF5TkhKT2JCazczd0VubGZORjFiU2FzTVp0bVNMcHptdGhxR0NmVUtXWllWaXNrN0JuTnhRTURPQVdnbmotd3dfcVM3cDdScXdxSld2T0tUOG53U05DOG13TW9DaWlibkxfMWR2THR0VzREcGF6bnowZ9IBuwFBVV95cUxQbmVUSzExUzBSV0hiNlkzRmFvX1Z6Z05ZQUlkcmJXNEpxWWpOVUJDRk40bThQTmRMcTNVYVZnMGdDbzI2YUFVVEl0R1d5OTBWU0pGX0xXOS1PS01aLWstMVVyWjhoNDg0b2dsLVNKUmlfeWFFODdPdWdaeV84R0YzMXZzWjctZUNVZlhXOE5EYzlMSG5hVG

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Bank of Korea Gov. Shin Hyun-song speaks during a parliamentary audit at the bank's headquarters in Seoul, Monday. Joint Press Corps
Bank of Korea (BOK) Gov. Shin Hyun-song said Monday that the central bank's August rate hike ahead of the U.S. Federal Reserve's September move gave Korea more time to respond and helped minimize the economic costs of monetary tightening.
"We signaled a shift toward higher interest rates earlier than many other major economies. By raising rates in August, ahead of the Fed's September hike, we bought ourselves time," Shin said during a parliamentary audit of the central bank.
"Taking action early carries far lower economic costs than having to raise rates defensively in response to market pressure," he added.
Shin said the preemptive move also helped guide market expectations, noting that the prospect of BOK's rate increases was reflected in bond yields and other market prices before the hikes actually took effect.
The BOK raised its policy rate by 0.25 percentage point in both July and August, bringing it to 3 percent. The Fed raised its benchmark rate by 0.25 percentage point in September, its first increase since July 2023, to a range of 3.75 percent to 4 percent.
During the audit, Shin left the door open to further rate hikes, warning that inflation is expected to remain above the BOK's 2 percent target for an extended period as cost pressures continue to pass through to consumer prices.
"One point we particularly take into account is that inflation hurts low income and vulnerable groups the most," Shin said in response to a question from Rep. Yoon Hu-duk of the ruling Democratic Party of Korea about possible rate hikes at the BOK's October and November policy meetings.
"The timing and pace of any further rate increases will depend on inflation and economic growth trends, as well as financial stability conditions," he said.
The governor also acknowledged that monetary tightening places a heavier burden on vulnerable households, as borrowers from lower income groups are hit harder by rate hikes.
The audit also saw opposition lawmakers question whether the government's expansionary fiscal policy would undermine the BOK's efforts to curb inflation through monetary tightening.
"Expansionary fiscal policy puts pressure on prices and the money supply in the short term," said Rep. Lee Jong-wook of the main opposition People Power Party.
Higher interest rates are intended to cool the economy by making borrowing more expensive and discouraging spending and investment. An expansionary fiscal policy, however, can have the opposite effect by injecting money into the economy and supporting demand.
In response, Shin said the BOK was using economic models to assess whether the two policies would work against each other.
He estimated that the government's budget proposal for next year would boost Korea's economic growth by 0.5 percentage point while adding 0.06 percentage point to inflation.
"If potential growth rises over the medium to long term, the two policies may not necessarily work against each other," Shin said.
Q. Why did the Bank of Korea raise interest rates before the U.S. Federal Reserve?
Q. Will the Bank of Korea raise interest rates again?
Q. How could South Korea’s expansionary fiscal policy affect inflation and economic growth?

</details>

### 30. ★★★☆☆ Trump faces growing backlash over plans to import diesel from Russia

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World / BBC Business / GN Top
- **多源确认**: 3
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T23:18:45+00:00
- **发生了什么**: Both Republican and Democratic lawmakers have criticised the deal, which was announced weeks before the US midterm elections.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Image caption, Global fuel prices have soared since the US-Israel war with Iran began at the end of February
US President Donald Trump is facing a growing backlash over plans to import diesel from Russia as the Iran war continues to impact global energy prices.
Trump on Friday announced that Russian President Vladimir Putin had agreed to release 300,000 tonnes of diesel immediately "to the American and global marketplace" followed by several other shipments.
That is despite signing legislation to authorise fresh US sanctions and tariffs on nations that import Russian oil and gas just weeks ago.
The move has been criticised by some Republican and Democratic lawmakers, who believe the deal violates US laws.
As well as the initial release of 300,000 tonnes of diesel, Trump said Russia would then supply 500,000 tonnes in November and one million more after that.
This would be followed by a further three million tonnes, which would be delivered "within a short period" dependent on the condition of its refineries, which have been attacked by Ukraine during the ongoing war between the two countries.
"Lower prices for Americans, especially our Great Farmers, Ranchers, and Truckers, is my Greatest Priority," Trump wrote in a Truth Social post.
Russia&#x27;s Deputy Prime Minister Alexander Novak confirmed these numbers to Tass, the country&#x27;s state-owned news agency, saying the export volume could reach three million tonnes per month in future.
Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky has called the deal a gift to Putin that amounts to "an investment in a war that must be ended".
It is understood the US Treasury is suspending sanctions on Russian diesel exports until 7 April as part of the deal, but other assets like those in American banks are still frozen.
A group of seven senators - four Republicans and three Democrats â on Sunday said the deal violated US laws, urging Trump to "reverse course".
"The United States should not provide Putin with a financial lifeline while Russia continues to kill Ukrainian civilians," the lawmakers said.
They added that Congress had "explicitly banned Russian energy imports in 2022 to prevent American dollars from financing Putin&#x27;s brutal war against Ukraine".
The statement was signed by Republicans Susan Collins, Lisa Murkowski, Thom Tillis and John Curtis, as well as Democrats Jeanne Shaheen, Richard Blumenthal and Ron Wyden.
Trump&#x27;s &#x27;energy ceasefire&#x27; claim welcomed by Zelensky but Russia silent
Trump wants to reduce the cost of fuel as the midterms loom - will it work?
Analysis: Trump&#x27;s shock Russia deal highlights mounting pressure to curb fuel prices
Not everyone is opposed to the deal. Republican Thomas Massie, who has sparred with Trump a number of issues, said the US should lift sanctions on all petroleum products.
Trump&#x27;s Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent told Fox News the agreement would work through existing legal channels the US has to help bring prices down and that anyone in Congress who said it would not was wrong.
Global fuel prices have soared since the US-Israel war with Iran began at the end of February, with the fighting severely disrupting supplies of crude oil - a key ingredient in petrol and diesel.
The rising prices have become a key issue for Americans ahead of next month&#x27;s midterms elections, which will determine if Trump&#x27;s Republican Party maintains control of Congress.
Polls show a majority of Americans disapprove of his handling of the economy and the war in Iran and he has been searching for ways to ease his party&#x27;s political pain.
The toll the war is taking has been acutely felt by US agricultural states, which rely heavily on diesel to operate farming machinery and to transport their products to market.
The average diesel price in the US is currently $6.28 (Â£4.74) a gallon, according to the AAA, down from the record high of $6.53 recorded at the end of September.
Analysts have questioned whether Russia is capable of providing that quantity of diesel and the extent to which doing so will ease prices for American consumers.
The US uses approximately 3.6 million barrels of diesel a day, so even if Russia delivers its promised 300,000 tonnes this month, it amounts to approximately half of the US daily consumption.
It is not only at home that Trump is facing opposition to his diesel deal with Russia. A number of international leaders have criticised it, including Canadian Prime Minister Mark Carney and UK Defence Secretary Wes Streeting, who said the US and other allies of Ukraine should focus on ramping up pressure on Russia to end the war.
Meanwhile, Trump announced in Sunday that Russia and Ukraine had reached an "energy ceasefire" but did not give more details. Ukrainian President Volodymyr Zelensky welcomed this but there was no response from Moscow.
UK does not support Trump&#x27;s Russia diesel deal, says Streeting
Trump announces deal for Russian diesel as Zelensky calls it a gift to Putin

</details>

### 31. ★★★☆☆ Ukraine strikes Yandex data centre in third attack on Russian tech giant

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: BBC World / BBC Business
- **多源确认**: 2
- **时间**: 2026-10-11T14:38:10+00:00
- **发生了什么**: It is the third time this week that Ukraine has targeted Russian tech giant Yandex, forcing the company to suspend its operations.

<details>
<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

Image caption, Yandex&#x27;s data centres have been targeted three times this week - like this one in Sasovo on Thursday
Yandex has been forced to pause its operations at a data centre near Moscow after the facility was damaged in a Ukrainian drone strike - the third attack on the Russian tech giant&#x27;s sites this week.
The company - dubbed "Russia&#x27;s Google" - said no one was injured at the centre in Vladimir, east of the capital, but some of its services were unavailable to users.
Earlier this week, Yandex said it was struggling to maintain its services after two of its five data centres were hit in Ukrainian strikes.
Both Russia and Ukraine have stepped up attacks on infrastructure in recent months as the war between them continues in its fifth year following Russia&#x27;s full-scale invasion.
Kyiv said it was targeting places that provide revenue streams for Moscow&#x27;s war machine, and that the attacks could force Russia to divert its resources to repair the damage at Yandex.
Sunday&#x27;s attack came hours after US President Donald Trump warned that Ukraine should "damn well stop" attacking Russia&#x27;s oil refineries so Moscow could release more diesel to the global market - a deal that has drawn criticism from Europe and sparked a diplomatic row with Ukraine&#x27;s President Volodymyr Zelensky.
&#x27;Time for Ukraine to get new president,&#x27; says Trump after Zelensky condemns diesel deal
Analysis: Trump&#x27;s shock Russia deal highlights mounting pressure to curb fuel prices
Yandex confirmed on Telegram that its data centre in Vladimir had been damaged and that operations had been "completely suspended", although it said it was working to restore connectivity.
The other strikes against Yandex facilities were on Thursday in Sasovo and Friday in Kaluga, south-west of Moscow, causing problems for some of its services, such as its AI chatbot and smart home devices.
The tech company - which also runs a food-ordering and taxi app used by tens of millions of people - said at the time that its data centres were critical for the normal operation of its services, and that it was "very difficult to predict" when they would be back online.
Meanwhile, Russian strikes on Ukraine have continued through the weekend.
The State Emergency Service of Ukraine said six people were injured in Russian attacks overnight on four districts in its south-eastern Dnipropetrovsk region.
On Saturday, at least 20 people including four children were killed in Russian attacks on Zaporizhzhia, local officials confirmed.
Russia has previously also struck Ukrainian data centres, causing internet outages and prompting some firms to move their servers abroad.

### 35. ★★★☆☆ How China and America Can Move from Competition to Stability - War on the Rocks

- **分类**: 🌍 地缘政治
- **来源**: GN China
- **多源确认**: 1
- **时间**: 2026-10-12T07:34:06+00:00
- **发生了什么**: How China and America Can Move from Competition to Stability&nbsp;&nbsp;War on the Rocks

<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

How China and America Can Move from Competition to Stability
Join War on the Rocks and gain access to content trusted by policymakers, military leaders, and strategic thinkers worldwide.
How China and America Can Move from Competition to Stability
How China and America Can Move from Competition to Stability
The American team thought it was signaling restraint. The Chinese team saw preparations for combat. In a closed-door Sino-American crisis simulation I helped organize in the 2010s &mdash; which the U.S. military funded as part of a long-term project &mdash; the Americans repositioned an aircraft carrier away from a maritime dispute between China and the Philippines, expecting the move to reassure Beijing. Instead, the Chinese participants became deeply alarmed. They believed the carrier was now ideally positioned to launch combat operations while remaining less vulnerable to attack. The episode revealed how suspicion could turn an intended reassurance into an apparent threat. This danger becomes more intense when each side views the other primarily through the lens of strategic competition. For nearly a decade , strategic competition has framed the U.S. approach to China, shaping military planning and operations, alliance diplomacy, trade and technology relations, investment policy, and &mdash; increasingly &mdash; scientific, educational, and cultural exchanges.
While strengthening deterrence and inspiring improvements in America's competitive capabilities, strategic competition has become an end in itself, encouraging dangerous policies often based on rigid, worst-case assumptions about Chinese intentions and abilities. And the same dynamic operates on the Chinese side. The framework fails to answer a central question: What kind of stable and beneficial U.S.-Chinese relationship is such competition intended to produce?
Both sides require greater reassurance, positive incentives to exercise restraint and cooperation, and recognition of the need for an enduring, stable balance supported by substantive, lasting agreements. Without these, stress on strategic competition is likely to produce recurring crises, accelerating arms competition, economic and technological fragmentation, and even a full-scale war, potentially involving nuclear weapons and most likely over Taiwan. Even without such a catastrophic outcome, strategic competition will almost certainly diminish cooperation on potentially existential, global threats such as climate change and the disruptive effects of emerging technologies like AI.
The 2026 National Defense Strategy states that the United States does not seek to "dominate," "strangle," or "humiliate" China. It also describes the American objective as an Indo-Pacific balance of power that allows a "decent peace," and stresses homeland and Western Hemisphere security. It also does not mention Taiwan, a major potential source of conflict. Such wording suggests the Trump administration is moving away from intense strategic competition backed by American superiority.
And yet, the same document calls for a strong denial defense against China along the first island chain extending from the Korean Peninsula to Southeast Asia, as well as an overall " favorable balance of power" in the Asia-Pacific, implying a continued need to geographically contain China through superior military power, as opposed to a stable balance based on mutual denial capabilities. The strategy does not offer reciprocal political limits or an agreed end-state for the relationship. And U.S. force modernization, alliance cooperation, and technology restrictions remain heavily shaped by concerns about a threatening China.
Despite the positive rhetoric occurring in parts of the National Defense Strategy and during both of this year's meetings between President Donald Trump and General Secretary Xi Jinping , U.S. policy remains a largely zero-sum competitive struggle requiring clearly superior, if not entirely dominant, military power to contain or constrain Beijing. The emphasis remains on "winning" a competition over creating a stable, beneficial outcome. As a result, the relationship still lacks an operational political framework for the "decent peace" called for by the United States.
Washington should pursue a strategy that bounds bilateral competition within a relationship that protects both countries' vital interests while reducing conflict risks and expanding opportunities for positive-sum outcomes. This process is best described by the Chinese concept endorsed by both leaders during the Beijing summit: constructive strategic stability. In an attempt to define this concept, Beijing has articulated broad principles and both governments have announced specific cooperative initiatives, but neither has publicly set out a coherent, sequenced framework linking specific political assurances to verifiable reciprocal restraint measures and, ultimately, durable agreements.
The Illogic and the Limits of Strategic Competition
There are many reasons why a policy of strategic competition does not serve American (or Chinese) interests.
First, it offers no clear, stable, mutually beneficial political end-state. Instead, the United States largely measures success through stronger military deployments, tighter anti-China alliances, export and investment controls, tariffs, and other restrictions. Under this approach, competition becomes self-reinforcing, as Chinese trade and technological gains invite additional restrictions, military modernization justifies larger deployments and increased defense spending, and diplomatic or political advances prompt zero-sum counters. Much the same set of responses is seen on the Chinese side.
Second, as a result, this approach encourages both sides to securitize nearly every aspect of the relationship, from trade and technology to scientific exchanges. As a result, an increasing number of activities by either side become subject to intense restrictions or prohibitions for real and imagined "national security" reasons.
Third, this dynamic deepens mistrust and encourages worst-case planning. The trajectory points toward either a competition for predominance or an unstable balance in which both sides constantly seek leverage, increasing the danger of military confrontation and conflict. In such an environment, procedural "guardrails" will prove insufficient unless the two governments also reach substantive, reciprocal arrangements that reduce the underlying sources of conflict.
Fourth, while any lasting strategy requires realistic assessments of each side's capabilities, strategic competition encourages inflated views of both the challenges each side poses and each side's ability to counter them, producing unrealistic expectations.
China is a formidable economic, technological, and military power and at times uses its capabilities to counter U.S. interests, including by restricting critical-mineral exports to the United States and, according to U.S. intelligence and cybersecurity agencies, penetrating U.S. critical-infrastructure networks to prepare for disruption during a crisis or conflict. It also undermines U.S. interests by supporting both Russia and Iran with military and dual-use components in their respective conflicts with Ukraine and the United States. But such actions do not justify portraying Beijing as an existential threat in other than nuclear warfare terms, or assuming its growth and influence in every case come at America's expense, necessitating a U.S. drive for dominance or clear superiority and zero-sum pushback on every front.
Despite efforts to reduce its vulnerability to outside pressure and its desire to stress state sovereignty and collective rights over liberal democratic ideas, China remains deeply integrated into the international economy, from which it has benefited and to which it has contributed significantly . Moreover, China faces structural constraints on its ability to achieve global normative and power dominance, such as adverse demographics, high debt and property sector weakness, and slowing productivity and growth. It also suffers under ideological constraints and a political system with limited international appeal .
While posing a military challenge to U.S. maritime predominance in the Western Pacific that requires sustained balancing, Beijing is highly unlikely to be able to assemble the capabilities needed for global military dominance in the foreseeable future &mdash; if ever &mdash; including extensive alliances, overseas bases , worldwide logistics, and broad international acceptance of Chinese leadership.
For its part, the United States is constrained in sustaining its historical predominance by steadily growing fiscal pressures, political polarization, public resistance to military escalation and prolonged wars (evident in polling on Iran), less-compliant allies , and a broader diffusion of global power .
Finally, open-ended strategic competition distorts American priorities and damages prosperity and long-term strength. The administration's budget proposals prioritize defense spending while reducing overall funding for civilian programs, weakening investment in the domestic foundations of competitiveness and national well-being.
The Alternative: What Constructive Strategic Stability Means and Where It Came From
In rejecting strategic competition, America should not return to engagement and hedging . While producing decades of relative stability, mutually beneficial economic interaction, and normalized exchanges, that policy downplayed unavoidable competition and became associated with unrealistic expectations of Chinese political liberalization and near-complete acceptance of the U.S.-led international system. Alhough hedging was arguably a logical form of insurance against a more aggressive China, Washington never fully defined, delimited, and implemented the concept. Moreover, as with strategic competition, neither notion defined a sustainable end-state for the relationship.
The term "strategic stability" has its roots in Cold War efforts to limit the dangers of U.S.-Soviet nuclear competition. Its current usage is much broader, however, referring to the overall Sino-American relationship. American officials such as Secretary of State Marco Rubio have employed "strategic stability" in this broader sense. But the full term, "constructive strategic stability," was apparently first used by Xi Jinping and other Chinese leaders. They defined a relationship marked by cooperation as the foundation, with moderate competition, manageable differences, and a durable peace. The American side then accepted the full term (not necessarily China's definition), adding " &hellip;on the basis of fairness and reciprocity ."
The Chinese origin and definition of constructive strategic stability might make some observers cautious, but that is not a reason to reject it. The test should be whether Washington defines the term and accepts it in ways that protect and advance American interests and constrain dangerous behavior on both sides. Washington should resist any definition equating the concept to one-sided respect for Beijing's red lines.
As I use the term, constructive strategic stability denotes both a political process and an outcome: a stable, mutually advantageous relationship and orientation to the international order. It assumes that neither the United States nor China is an existential threat to the other in every day-to-day circumstance outside the extreme case of a nuclear war, nor on a path to inevitable decline or dominance of one over the other. It recognizes that both, through their interaction, create dangerous, virtually unbounded competition. And it posits that global and national order and prosperity require extensive bilateral cooperation and mutual benefit, which necessitate substantive accommodation and reciprocal restraint.
Some level of rivalry and competition are unavoidable, given the two powers' contrasting political systems and search for security and prosperity amid scarce resources. Nonetheless, Beijing and Washington should not allow rivalry to overshadow the obvious need to create lasting stability in their relationship and address those major, sometimes existential threats demanding cooperation, including climate warming, pandemics, AI governance, global financial stability, and nuclear conflict.
Building constructive strategic stability should not depend on a severe, restraint-inducing crisis or different, more restraint-oriented political leaders. The present administration has provided the political language needed to begin this process, including the National Defense Strategy's call for strategic stability and a "decent peace" with China, the Beijing summit's endorsement of a constructive relationship of strategic stability based on fairness and reciprocity, and similar comments made during Xi's September 2026 visit to Washington.
The political constituency for this relationship, in both the United States and China, could begin with officials who favor strong deterrence but want to avoid an open-ended crisis and address common concerns, as well as military professionals interested in reducing the risk of accidental escalation and conflict. It could also include business and technology constituencies that favor a predictable, profitable relationship and resist its expanding securitization.
Constructive strategic stability cannot emerge through a single summit, a comprehensive "grand bargain," or declarations of goodwill. Nor can it rest on unilateral concessions followed by expectations of reciprocity. It should develop gradually through concrete, mutually beneficial, verifiable interactions.
The purpose of such interactions is to test intentions through behavior, convert successful exchanges into lasting institutionalized commitments, and move from zero-sum strategic competition with guardrails toward reciprocal restraint, negotiated accommodation, and binding cooperation that does far more than just avoid war.
This effort thus moves beyond an argument I made in War on the Rocks in 2024 about the inadequacy of existing crisis-management mechanisms, and revisited after the May summit in June 2026. Improving crisis management should be only one step toward a basic shift in the relationship's framework.
The process should move through four overlapping stages: political communication, operational reassurance, reciprocal restraint, and institutionalization. These stages should cover three main policy arenas: Taiwan and Asian security; economics and technology; and nuclear, cyber, and AI. Early measures are to be limited, verifiable, and generally reversible. More consequential steps should come only after less costly ones have shown their value. Failure in one area should not destroy cooperation elsewhere.
Communicate Clear Political Boundaries and Test Reciprocity
The August 2022 crisis following then-U.S. House Speaker Nancy Pelosi's visit to Taiwan illustrated the danger of contradictory U.S. policy signals without clearly understood political boundaries. Pelosi, a highly provocative figure in U.S.-Chinese relations, took what her office described as an " official visit " to Taiwan, despite the supposedly unofficial nature of the U.S. relationship with the island. The crisis demonstrated the need to communicate consistently and clearly what political actions are deemed intolerable and what assurances are expected.
The first step in building constructive strategic stability should thus be an authoritative (perhaps joint) statement by the two leaders defining the relationship they desire and the dangers they intend to avoid. It should clearly state that neither will seek the strategic subordination, economic collapse, or political weakening or disintegration of the other, and that both nations will remain to some significant extent mutually dependent to ensure mutual benefit.
Regarding Taiwan, Trump has moved partway toward these assurances. After the Beijing summit, he clearly reemphasized a longstanding U.S. policy by publicly cautioning against a Taiwan declaration of independence, while declining to say &mdash; in contrast to Biden 's remark &mdash; whether the United States would defend the island and stating that he had made no commitment to Xi regarding Taiwan. However, Trump also created concern in Taiwan and the United States by questioning the viability of defending the island and casting doubt on a pending arms sale package.
In place of this ambiguity, the president should consistently provide a balanced set of deterrence and assurance signals, such as: The United States will oppose any unilateral attempts by either side to change the status quo in fundamental ways, toward either independence or unification, as defined and interpreted by Washington. This would go beyond the existing " do not support " statement of U.S. policy regarding Taiwan's independence, to convey Washington's clear and strong resolve to actively oppose such an action when necessary. Such attempts could include a formal effort by Taipei to endorse or codify a One China, One Taiwan policy, or, regarding China and unification, a direct Chinese use of force (however limited) against Taiwan.
Equally important, the Trump administration should also clearly reverse efforts to undermine the One China policy and unambiguously reaffirm its commitment to that policy . Specifically, the president should state that he will not place combat units on the island or take other actions suggesting a military alliance with Taipei and will emphasize only defensive capabilities in U.S. assistance to Taiwan. He should also clearly assert, as the One China policy stipulates , that Washington remains open to any peaceful, uncoerced resolution of the Taiwan issue acceptable to people on both sides of the strait, and should press Taipei to stop undermining that policy by portraying Taiwan as a vital U.S. strategic asset that, by implication, must remain separate from China. Even Taiwan's possession of high-technology semiconductor fabrication capabilities does not justify such a portrayal.
On the Chinese side, Xi Jinping should reiterate unambiguously that peaceful unification remains China's preferred objective, state unequivocally that there is no fixed timetable for unification, and assert a willingness to significantly reduce military activities around Taiwan as part of a larger tension-reduction effort.
Although unlikely to end distrust, these baseline declarations would establish political benchmarks for behavior and test both sides' willingness to engage in reciprocal assurances and accommodations.
Reinforce Political Assurances with Operational Activities
Political assurances have little value unless they shape the conduct of military and other instruments of state power.
On Oct. 24, 2023, a Chinese J-11 fighter intercepted an American B-52 over the South China Sea and, according to U.S. Indo-Pacific Command, passed within roughly 10 feet at night, creating a serious collision risk. This was not an isolated incident . Unsafe encounters have continued involving U.S. allies: According to Australia's Department of Defence, a Chinese naval helicopter maneuvered dangerously close to an Australian helicopter over the Yellow Sea on March 4, 2026, forcing its crew to take evasive action. The 2023 incident showed the limitations of a 2014 memorandum and a 2015 air-to-air safety annex on safe U.S.-Chinese air and maritime encounters, whose rules have not consistently prevented unsafe encounters.
Washington could propose a 90-day trial period &mdash; following the authoritative presidential assurances outlined above and marked by immediate, reciprocal obligations that both militaries avoid unsafe encounters &mdash; in which the two sides would routinely employ designated emergency communications channels, investigate reported violations within an agreed period, and exchange advance notification of unusually large exercises and major missile tests.
During the same period, the Pentagon could conduct a classified review, without yet implementing its recommendations, of close-in reconnaissance missions to determine whether a significant number could be conducted at greater distance, lower frequency, or with different platforms without materially reducing intelligence collection.
None of these steps would require Washington to abandon deterrence, change its one-China policy, curtail lawful military operations, or concede a Chinese red line.
During the 90-day trial period, Chinese forces would have to consistently meet the above observable benchmarks before Washington proceeded to reconnaissance reductions. If Beijing persistently failed to meet these benchmarks while insisting that Washington alone must demonstrate "respect," the initiative should stop at the reassurance stage, without the United States having altered its military posture.
In economics and technology, Washington and Beijing should work both independently and together to distinguish genuine security requirements from commercial competition. Each should explain the national-security rationale behind major technology and investment restrictions and consult, when feasible, before enacting new ones. Although dual-use capabilities present challenges, more can be done to reverse expanding securitization. Both sides should evaluate technologies with possible indirect applications against transparent, mutually understood criteria.
In addition, the two governments should establish regular, results-oriented dialogues on industrial subsidies, investment screening, intellectual-property protection, scientific cooperation, and AI, as well as cyber and nuclear operations. The latter three areas ought to include procedures for notifying one another of major incidents that appear accidental or unintended. These dialogues would identify red lines and opportunities for mutual assurances and sustained cooperation.
Finally, the two sides should strengthen crisis-prevention and management mechanisms through an official military and civilian Track One dialogue supported by unofficial Track Two discussions addressing both the political and military sources of crises and the guidelines and communication channels needed to manage them. My recently published crisis management manual addresses these and other issues in detail.
Move From Reassurance to Concrete Restraint Measures
The economic and technological relationship between Beijing and Washington demonstrates why reassurance should eventually produce concrete restraint. On Oct. 7, 2022 , Washington announced sweeping controls on China's access to advanced computing chips and semiconductor-manufacturing equipment, and subsequently expanded those restrictions. Beijing responded by requiring licenses for exports of materials important to semiconductor, defense, and battery supply chains.
Both governments invoked national security, but neither defined a mutually understood limiting principle, a process for consultation, or conditions under which they might narrow or remove restrictions. The result was a self-reinforcing cycle in which each side's attempt to reduce vulnerability increased the other's incentive to retaliate. Constructive strategic stability must therefore move beyond verbal reassurance and temporary "truces" to reciprocal, verifiable, and enduring restraints on the activities each side finds most destabilizing.
In security, after Beijing has demonstrated consistent compliance during the 90-day military-safety trial outlined above, Washington could implement the classified review's recommendations to reduce close-in reconnaissance operations, especially those providing marginal intelligence value relative to their political and crisis-instability costs. These adjustments would preserve essential deterrent and intelligence capabilities and remain reversible.
Beijing would have to sustain safe intercept behavior and measurably reduce coercive military activities around Taiwan (including frequent median-line crossings, large-scale encirclement exercises, and simulated blockade operations) before further steps, such as reciprocal exercise observations, are taken. Washington could invite Chinese observers to selected military exercises, including the major multinational Rim of the Pacific exercise, subject to applicable statutory restrictions. Beijing could reciprocate U.S. invitations by inviting American observers to People's Liberation Army exercises.
Economic and technology policies should reduce securitization, with Washington narrowing or removing those export controls whose demonstrated security benefits do not justify their costs to civilian production, research, and beneficial exchange. Restrictions affecting semiconductor manufacturing and biotechnology equipment deserve particular scrutiny, with controls tailored to specific security risks. Beijing should narrow politically motivated market access restrictions, strengthen protections against forced technology transfer , reduce critical mineral export controls , and provide more equal treatment for foreign companies in China .
In nuclear, cyber, and AI policy, Washington and Beijing should curtail, if not end, provocative operations that raise fears of nuclear escalation, removal of human control over nuclear use, or attacks on critical civilian infrastructure. As verification capabilities develop, they should negotiate a formal cyber noninterference declaration prohibiting peacetime attacks against nuclear command-and-control systems, civilian electrical grids, hospitals, and other critical civilian infrastructure, while establishing incident-notification and consultation procedures. And they should establish a clear link and protocol for the AI-incident communication channel created when Xi Jinping visited Washington.
The 2015 cyber agreement illustrates both the value and the fragility of institutionalized restraint. President Barack Obama and Xi agreed that neither government would conduct or knowingly support cyber-enabled theft of intellectual property for commercial advantage. Independent cybersecurity researchers reported a marked decline in intrusions attributed to China-based groups beginning in mid-2014, even before the agreement. But the improvement proved difficult to sustain as enforcement remained uncertain and the broader relationship deteriorated.
The episode suggests that restraint in any policy area becomes durable only when a commitment defines the prohibited behavior, establishes a baseline and reliable means of assessing compliance, requires regular review, and specifies proportionate consequences for violations without allowing one dispute to destroy the entire arrangement. The goal is not to make cheating impossible. It's to make noncompliance visible enough that reciprocal restraint remains politically defensible.
In this entire process, verification would vary by issue. U.S. and allied surveillance, commercial satellite imagery, ship and aircraft tracking, and agreed exercise notifications can reveal military behavior around Taiwan. Published regulations, licensing data, customs data, and company experience can track export controls and critical-mineral restrictions. Because responsibility for cyber violations is harder to establish, initial arrangements should focus on incident consultation, protection of clearly defined categories of infrastructure, and patterns of behavior, rather than unverifiable prohibitions. These verifications can have a preceding 12-month baseline, involve a sustained level of compliance across successive review periods, and and draw on a U.S. interagency assessment on the American side, ideally involving American allies. Such a process should also exist on the Chinese side.
As confidence develops and tensions abate, both governments should eventually reach parallel, independently adopted restraints limiting particularly destabilizing military capabilities, including certain intermediate-range missile deployments, offensive antisatellite systems, large concentrations of missile forces opposite Taiwan, and U.S. defense assistance to the island. As always, American adjustments should occur only as Chinese restraint becomes substantial and verifiable.
A bilateral economic security agreement should define restricted technologies, specify a process of consultation before major restrictions, and codify regular reviews of export controls, sanctions, tariffs, investment screening, and critical-mineral policies. Each action should specify its objective, criteria for success, and conditions for lifting or escalating controls.
As suggested above, formal cyber and AI agreements should take place at this stage, establishing reliable consultation mechanisms and protections against attacks on nuclear command-and-control and critical civilian infrastructure. The two nations should also restore mutually beneficial scientific, educational, and technological exchanges and protect them from short-term political fluctuations.
In all of the above stages, close consultation with America's allies and key partners should occur to the extent possible, including Taiwan. Washington should explain the need to move from open-ended strategic competition to a stability-centered, mutually beneficial relationship requiring clear agreements and understandings with Beijing, based in part on concessions and accommodations. Washington should make it clear that these agreements would not undermine the security of other countries. Indeed, a stable bilateral relationship of mutual benefit would bolster region-wide security.
Washington must also consult Congress from the outset and at each stage along the way. The executive branch could initiate the military-safety trial period under existing authority , but later measures &mdash; especially adjustments involving congressionally mandated economic restrictions or U.S. arms policy toward Taiwan &mdash; should not occur solely through executive action. Early and consistent consultation would make subsequent steps more politically defensible and durable, reducing the risk that commitments would collapse with a change of administration.
Agreements on reciprocal military restraint regarding Taiwan could appear to violate the Six Assurances and the Taiwan Relations Act . While the Taiwan Relations Act is a law, the Six Assurances are legally non-binding policy commitments. Neither rule out voluntary U.S.-Chinese understandings to reduce military tensions. That said, adjustments in arms assistance must meet the act's requirements. The act requires defense articles and services sufficient to maintain Taiwan's self-defense capability, with their nature and quantity determined by the president and Congress "based solely upon their judgment of the needs of Taiwan." It also requires review of those needs by U.S. military authorities. Following this wording, verified reductions in the Chinese threat through the above-outlined mutual tension reduction process could lower Taiwan's defense requirements and thus justify independently determined adjustments in U.S. assistance.
The Six Assurances state that the United States " Has not agreed to consult with the PRC on arms sales to Taiwan." This wording does not literally prohibit future understandings. Moreover, the accompanying 1982 cables also contemplated reductions in arms sales conditioned on continued Chinese peaceful behavior, with Taiwan's defense needs remaining the U.S. benchmark. Any adjustment should therefore be an independent, reversible American response to verified Chinese restraint, without instituting prior consultations with Beijing over specific arms sales or granting it control over Taiwan's defenses.
Constructive strategic stability is not a call to blindly trust Beijing, abandon deterrence, or accept a Chinese sphere of influence. It is an attempt to embed competition and deterrence within a stability-centered political framework and test whether limited reassurance can produce reciprocal restraint and enduring agreements. If China refuses meaningful reciprocity, the United States loses little from low-cost, reversible measures and gains evidence about Beijing's intentions. If China responds, both gain a basis for larger steps.
Building constructive strategic stability will require sustained political leadership in both countries and, to the fullest extent possible, support from U.S. allies. Neither Washington nor Beijing can create a stable relationship unilaterally. Each must demonstrate through repeated behavior that it can defend its own vital interests while recognizing that the other possesses legitimate interests that require accommodation.
The choice is therefore not whether the United States will compete with China. Competition is unavoidable. The choice is whether that competition becomes an increasingly self-sustaining struggle for predominance or operates within a broader political strategy designed to produce a stable, beneficial peace over the long term.
That distinction could determine far more than who wins the next round of U.S.-Chinese competition. It could determine whether the world's two most powerful countries learn to live with one another at all.
Michael D. Swaine is a senior research fellow at the Quincy Institute for Responsible Statecraft. Formerly a senior fellow at the Carnegie Endowment for International Peace and the RAND Corporation, Swaine is a specialist in Chinese defense and foreign policy, U.S.-Chinese relations, and East Asian international relations. His work has focused on Sino-American crisis management and the Taiwan issue. He has authored and edited more than a dozen books and monographs as well as many journal articles and book chapters in these areas. Swaine directs security-related projects with Chinese partners and advises the U.S. government on Asian security issues. He received his M.A. and Ph.D. in government from Harvard University.

<summary><strong>原文 / 抓取到的正文</strong></summary>

For the better part of the last four years, Wall Street&#x27;s bull market has been unstoppable. Since early June, the time-tested Dow Jones Industrial Average ( ^DJI +0.83% ) , benchmark S&P 500 ( ^GSPC +0.59% ) , and technology-driven Nasdaq Composite ( ^IXIC +0.64% ) have all blasted to fresh record highs.
Investors are finding no shortage of reasons to be excited about equities, including (but not limited to):
The artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure build-out
But as the popular idiom goes, "when things seem too good to be true, they usually are."
Image source: Getty Images. Image source: Getty Images.
While history can&#x27;t guarantee what&#x27;s to come, past events, more often than not, can foreshadow the future.
At the moment, the stock market is on the verge of doing something that no investor has witnessed in the last 156 years. Based on previous milestones leading to this point , trouble (and potentially a stock market crash) may be brewing.
Stock market valuations are approaching uncharted territory
Just as there&#x27;s a laundry list of things going right for the stock market, there&#x27;s also a long list of headwinds threatening to end Wall Street&#x27;s AI-driven rally, such as rapidly rising margin debt , persistently elevated inflation, and surging long-duration bond yields.
But based solely on history, there&#x27;s perhaps nothing more frightening for investors than premium stock valuations.
Defining "value" is tricky because there isn&#x27;t a blueprint for evaluating every public company or the broader market. When individual investors evaluate and value businesses, they incorporate some degree of emotion and/or subjectivity. The absence of a one-size-fits-all value approach is what makes accurately predicting short-term directional moves in the Dow, S&P 500, and Nasdaq Composite so challenging.
However, one time-tested valuation tool has done a remarkable job of providing investors with apples-to-apples comparisons of broad market valuations throughout history: the S&P 500&#x27;s Shiller P/E Ratio, also known as the Cyclically Adjusted P/E Ratio ( CAPE Ratio ).
What gives the Shiller P/E Ratio a leg up over all other valuation tools is that it&#x27;s based on average inflation-adjusted earnings over the last 10 years. Whereas earnings turning negative during recessions can make the traditional P/E ratio useless, this isn&#x27;t the case with the Shiller P/E Ratio.
Stock Market Shiller PE Ratio on the verge of taking out its Dot Com Bubble all-time high 🚨 🤯 👀 https://t.co/CtCmSgWnLt
Though economists introduced this valuation tool in 1988, it&#x27;s been backtested to January 1871, providing roughly 156 years of monthly valuation data. Over this time, the average Shiller P/E Ratio is a relatively modest 17.42. As of the closing bell on Oct. 5, the S&P 500&#x27;s Shiller P/E clocked in at 41.67. That&#x27;s within a stone&#x27;s throw of its all-time high of 44.19, set in December 1999.
While making history is normally a good thing for the stock market, making valuation history is the exact opposite , according to historical precedent.
The first time the CAPE Ratio surpassed 30 was in August-September 1929. This two-month period of investing euphoria on Wall Street was followed by the Great Depression. The Dow Jones Industrial Average lost 89% of its value from peak to trough during the Great Depression.
Mind you, this isn&#x27;t the only time the CAPE Ratio has surpassed 30 . The benchmark S&P 500 lost about 20% in the fourth quarter of 2018 after it topped 30, and the COVID-19 crash wiped away 34% of the S&P 500&#x27;s value in just 33 calendar days in February-March 2020.
The first time the CAPE Ratio exceeded 40 was during the internet-driven hype. Mere months after peaking at 44.19, the dot-com bubble burst. The S&P 500 and Nasdaq Composite had lost 49% and 78% of their respective values by October 2002.
Historical precedent isn&#x27;t subtle. When stock valuations soar to never-before-seen premiums, generational downtrends and/or stock market crashes occur. The Shiller P/E Ratio is on the verge of making dubious history and crossing above 44.19 for the first time.
The good news: History works both ways and strongly favors optimists
Even though the Shiller P/E Ratio can&#x27;t tell investors when the music will stop or what catalyst will pull the rug out from beneath a historically pricey stock market, the foreshadowing is crystal clear... over the short term. But when investors take a step back and examine the bigger picture, they&#x27;ll find an entirely different story .
There&#x27;s no question that stock market corrections, bear markets, and crashes can be scary and tug on investors&#x27; heartstrings. But these events share a common thread: they&#x27;re relatively short-lived.
In late May, the analysts at Bespoke Investment Group examined the length of 27 separate S&P 500 bull and bear market events since the start of the Great Depression in September 1929. Bespoke&#x27;s data set, published on X (formerly Twitter), highlights the night-and-day difference between optimism and pessimism on Wall Street.
In one corner, the average S&P 500 bear market reached its trough in 286 calendar days, or the equivalent of 9.5 months. Additionally, no bear market has lasted longer than 630 calendar days over the last 97 years.
The current bull market that began on 10/12/22 is now the 9th longest in S&amp;P 500 history, surpassing the 1,324-day bull that ended on 2/9/1966: https://t.co/4mGsS2t2ft
By comparison, the typical S&P 500 bull market has persisted for an average of 1,023 calendar days, or approximately 3.6 times as long as the average bear market. What&#x27;s more, 14 of 27 bull markets lasted longer than the aforementioned lengthiest bear market.
If nearly a century of research isn&#x27;t enough, the analysts at Crestmont Research have you covered. They calculated the rolling 20-year total returns, including dividends, of the broad-based S&P 500 since 1900. Mind you, the S&P wasn&#x27;t incepted until 1923, meaning researchers had to track the performance of its components in other major indexes from 1900 to 1923.
Crestmont&#x27;s data set produced 107 rolling 20-year periods (1900-1919, 1901-1920, and so on, through 2006-2025). What&#x27;s noteworthy is that all 107 generated a positive average annual total return .
Put another way, even if investors hypothetically purchased an S&P 500-tracking fund ahead of the Great Depression, the dot-com bubble, or other major downturns or crash events, their investment would have generated a positive total return every time if it had been held for 20 years .
Even if the stock market makes dubious valuation history, well over a century of total return data suggests that long-term, optimistic investors will be just fine.

### 37. ★★★☆☆ China sanctions Paraguay, Belize ministers over Taiwan ties

Share China sanctions Paraguay, Belize ministers over Taiwan ties on social media
Taiwan's flag is carried across the sky during National Day celebrations in Taipei, Taiwan, on October 10, 2025 [Ann Wang/Reuters]
China has sanctioned Paraguay’s communications minister and Belize’s foreign minister over ties to Taiwan.
The Chinese Ministry of Foreign Affairs made the announcement on Monday, citing the safeguarding of China’s national sovereignty and opposition to Taiwan separatist activities as the reasons behind the measures, which went into effect the same day.
list of 3 items list 1 of 3 Taiwan president says defence spending boost aims to ‘deter war’
list 2 of 3 What the Iran war has done to the world’s energy supply, in charts
list 3 of 3 North Korea fires multiple ballistic missiles towards the sea
The measures against Paraguay’s Communications Minister Gustavo Villate and Belize’s Foreign Minister Francis Fonseca include the freezing of their assets in China and the denial of entry visas to Hong Kong and Macau.
The sanctions also prohibit Chinese organisations and individuals from engaging in transactions, cooperation or other activities with the ministers.
The Foreign Ministry said that “certain anti-China politicians” who are “driven by self-interest” have pushed their nations to maintain diplomatic relations with Taiwan.
Beijing said the ministers had “taken a series of egregious actions and statements”, which are “damaging China’s core interests”.
Paraguay and Belize are two of 12 countries that maintain formal diplomatic relations with Taiwan.
China views Taiwan as its territory, a view the island’s government strongly rejects.
The measures come two days after Taiwan’s National Day celebration.
In a National Day speech outside the Presidential Office on Saturday, Taiwan President William Lai Ching-te said the island “cherishes peace” and will “not give up our freedom”.
China’s Taiwan Affairs Office condemned Lai’s speech, saying it “disregards facts, ⁠distorts the truth, misleads the public, and reveals ulterior motives”.
In January, Chinese President Xi Jinping pledged to achieve the “reunification” of China and Taiwan, calling the goal “unstoppable”.
Paraguay’s Communications Minister Villate marked Taiwan’s National Day with a social media post, saying: “Paraguay reaffirms its relationship of friendship and cooperation, based on shared values and principles: the self-determination of peoples, human rights and democracy.”
The Belize embassy in Taiwan also posted a message on its Facebook celebrating the 37th anniversary of their diplomatic relations.
“Together, we look forward to a future of growth, opportunity, and lasting friendship,” the embassy wrote.
Last month, Taiwan’s Foreign Minister Lin Chia-lung visited Belize. His ministry said the two sides had agreed to deepen ties.
Taiwan’s other allies in the Americas include Guatemala and Haiti. It also has formal diplomatic ties with three Caribbean islands.
The United States does not have ⁠formal diplomatic ties with Taiwan, but Washington is bound by law to provide the island with the means to ‌defend itself.
Earlier this month, the US delivered two fighter jets to Taiwan as the island seeks to build its defence capabilities to deter a feared attack from China .

### 41. ★★★☆☆ Here's What a $1,400 Investment in Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing (TSMC) Stock Could Be Worth in 3 Years - Yahoo Finance

Here's What a $1,400 Investment in Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing (TSMC) Stock Could Be Worth in 3 Years
Spending on artificial intelligence (AI) infrastructure is likely to keep ramping up over at least the next several years. Gartner projects AI infrastructure spending to reach nearly $2 trillion by 2027, while a Deloitte survey found that 87% of companies expected their AI infrastructure spending to at least double by 2028, with 30% expecting their AI budgets to be at least five times higher.
This should be a big boost to Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing (NYSE: TSM), which is the world's largest foundry (chip manufacturer). Manufacturing advanced logic chips, like graphics processing units (GPUs) and server-center central processing units (CPUs), is a difficult endeavor that requires both technological expertise and scale. As competitors have struggled to achieve high yields (few defects), TSMC has established itself as a virtual monopoly in advanced chipmaking and one of the most important companies in the semiconductor supply chain.
Missed Nvidia in 2009? This Rare Signal Is Flashing Again. In 2009, a "Double Down" signal flashed for a little-known chipmaker called Nvidia. For the first time in years, that same "Total Conviction" signal is flashing for a company 1/100th the size of Nvidia. Continue »
Image source: The Motley Fool. This also makes TSMC one of the best ways to play the AI infrastructure boom, in my opinion. With $1,400, you could buy three shares of the stock.
The great thing about TSMC is that it wins no matter which AI chip company takes market share. Whether it's Nvidia or Advanced Micro Devices with their GPUs, or Broadcom and Marvell Technology with their custom AI ASICs (application-specific integrated circuits), like Alphabet 's tensor processing units (TPUs), TSMC manufactures all of these chips.
The same holds largely true in the data center CPU market. Intel manufactures most of its own CPUs, although it is expected to lose market share in the coming years. Meanwhile, the CPU market as a whole is expected to explode, driven by the rise of agentic AI.
Beyond the data center market, TSMC will undoubtedly benefit from the proliferation of chips enabled by other technological advances, including robotics, self-driving automobiles, and physical AI. These are all potentially huge markets that will need a lot of advanced semiconductors.
In the meantime, TSMC is seeing outstanding revenue growth and strong gross margins . Last quarter, its revenue climbed nearly 34% in U.S. dollars to $40.2 billion, while its gross margin expanded 910 basis points to 67.7%. Revenue growth has accelerated in the third quarter, with July revenue climbing 45% in local currency, August revenue up 52%, and September sales surging 55%. TSMC is working closely with its largest customers to increase capacity to meet growing chip demand, while its position in the industry has also given it solid pricing power.
Given the boom in AI infrastructure spending and the continued proliferation of chips, analysts expect to see strong growth for TSMC over the next few years.
Analysts currently forecast that the company's adjusted earnings per share (EPS) will climb from $16.96 this year to $28.64 in 2028. Assume another 25% EPS growth the following year and 20% the year after that, and it would produce adjusted EPS of roughly $43 in 2030.
The stock currently trades at a forward price-to-earnings (P/E) of just over 21 times 2027 analyst estimates. If we apply an 18x to 22x forward P/E multiple to the stock, that would have it trading in a range of $775 to $950 in three years.
A less than $1,400 investment (three shares) would be worth between $2,325 and $2,850 in 2029. That's equivalent to roughly a 70% return at the low end and a more than 100% gain at the high end.
With TSCM stock potentially doubling over the next three years, it is a top AI stock to buy today. The biggest risks to the stock hitting these levels would be an AI infrastructure spending slowdown and/or the company losing market share and pricing power to a competitor like Samsung , Intel, or even the new Terrafab joint venture being built by Tesla and Space Exploration Technologies .
